AZOA — Federation Rails Engagement Model

Layer: 03 — Autonomous Zones of Action (workflows, identity, settlement; formerly the OASIS layer) Steward: AZOA project License: Open-source engine (Apache-2.0, self-hostable end to end); commercial tier = PrimusNeo-managed nodes, enterprise federation gateway, certified rail adapters, SLA-backed support Reference deployment: Tie Town [TX] cross-system fabric Anchors source: ../research/layer-comparables.md

1. What AZOA actually is (in the buyer’s vocabulary)

AZOA is an engine for financial workflows that are highly dynamic but strictly structured. You describe a multi-step process — who acts, what moves, under what conditions, in what order — and AZOA runs it durably to completion, settling value across whichever rails the workflow touches: blockchains, fiat, or both. Three composable layers make that work:

  • Identity — self-sovereign avatars a person actually owns (wallet-challenge login, ed25519), with consent grants that let an app act on their behalf only within explicit, revocable permission.
  • Quests — durable workflow graphs (gates, transfers, swaps, grants, external calls) that survive restarts, reconcile against real-world truth before acting again, and never double-spend.
  • Dapps (STAR) — a generator that scaffolds and deploys applications on the identity and quest layers, so a new product doesn’t rebuild the plumbing.

It still absorbs the integration tax the old interoperability pitch targeted — one structured seam instead of MuleSoft + chain-interop (Chainlink CCIP, LayerZero, Wormhole) + identity orchestration (Okta, Entra, Auth0, SSI Verified Credentials) point solutions — but the differentiated surface is workflow settlement: the same quest can move on-chain value (holons via ChainProvider adapters) and fiat-originated value (an idempotent, KYC-gated bridge call after money clears) in one run, with exactly-once execution, fail-closed security, and reconcile-before-acting discipline.

The pitch in one sentence: “What Temporal is to durable code and MuleSoft is to integration, AZOA is to money in motion — durable, auditable, consent-gated workflows that settle across chain and fiat.”

In the language enterprises already know:

  • A healthcare CIO: AZOA is the consent-gated identity and settlement seam that makes patient-owned records and payer settlement run on one auditable workflow.
  • A CSCO at a global 3PL: AZOA signs and settles milestones (loaded, sailed, cleared, delivered) so trade finance, insurance, and customs execute on the same evidence — without a TradeLens-shaped operator owning the network.
  • A utility CIO: AZOA is the settlement rail under flexibility dispatch — verified identity per asset, quest-gated payouts per MWh.
  • A bank Head of Digital Assets: AZOA is the multi-chain + fiat + legacy-core workflow engine where exactly-once and fail-closed are architectural guarantees, not middleware promises.
  • A DAO Steward Council: AZOA is how the DAO acts in the world — consent-delegated execution, structured treasury workflows, and rails to both chains and fiat partners.

AZOA is the layer that makes the federated four-layer stack a stack — the value-motion seam through which the other layers act on the world. SurrealDB is its sole data engine; real-world state (chain confirmations, settlement callbacks) is the source of truth, so balances are read, never stored.

2. License posture

Open-source engine + commercial managed tier. The entire AZOA engine — identity & consent, quests, holons, swaps, STAR, settlement bridges, the @azoa/sdk TypeScript SDK — is Apache-2.0 and self-hostable end to end. What we charge for is operational:

Component Commercial posture
AZOA engine (.NET 10 WebAPI, all protocol layers) Apache-2.0 — free to self-host, fork, extend
@azoa/sdk (TypeScript), ChainProvider / DexAdapter interfaces Apache-2.0
PrimusNeo-managed AZOA nodes (hosted WebAPI + SurrealDB, ops, upgrades) Commercial (PEL)
Enterprise federation gateway (multi-tenant, multi-node, SLA-bearing) Commercial (PEL)
Certified settlement-rail adapters (fiat partners, premium chain relationships carrying commercial cost) Commercial (PEL)
Enterprise support, SLAs, compliance evidence packs Commercial (PEL)

See ../02-licensing-model.md §3. Self-hosting is genuinely free — the moat is the managed service, certified rails, and trademark, not the source.

3. Pricing tiers (annual managed-tier license, before services)

Scale Definition Annual License Services Multiplier (Y1) Typical 3-yr TCO
Small <10 rails/integrations, single node, <1M API calls/month $24K–$60K/yr 2–3x $200K–$550K
Mid 10–50 rails/integrations, multi-chain + fiat, 1M–50M calls/month $80K–$250K/yr 2–3x $620K–$2.0M
Large 50+ rails/integrations, federation gateway, >50M API calls/month, cross-org $350K–$1.5M/yr 2–3x $2.7M–$12M

Pricing mechanic options (default in bold):

  • Per-rail + base platform fee (default; most predictable): base $24K–$200K/yr + $3K–$15K/rail-or-connector/yr beyond the included count (5/15/50 for Small/Mid/Large)
  • Per-API-call / per-quest-run metered overage: $0.001–$0.05/call beyond included tier volume (1M / 50M / unlimited+)
  • Per-vCore equivalent (MuleSoft-style, for buyers used to that mechanic): $1,000–$1,400/vCore/month
  • Per-entity-enrolled (federation deployments): $2K–$15K/entity/yr for governance-fabric pricing

Gas/chain fees (for on-chain settlement) are always pass-through at cost + 12% management fee, never bundled into the platform license. Fiat settlement-partner fees stay with the settlement tenant — AZOA never runs the checkout or owns the payout.

4. Services mix

AZOA is the highest services multiplier of the four layers (2–3x Y1) because rail and connector buildout is real engineering work. Typical Mid-tier SOW:

Service Scope Fixed-fee range
Integration audit + discovery Inventory of buyer’s existing integrations and settlement flows, prioritization $40K–$80K
ERP/MES/EHR/TMS/CRM connectors Per-connector fixed-fee (see ../03-services-catalog.md §2.2 connector class table) $20K–$400K/connector
Chain rails Per-chain via ChainProvider (Solana + Wormhole routes shipped; EVM et al. via the adapter interface) $20K–$75K/chain
Fiat settlement seams Per settlement tenant: KYC-gated bridge integration, idempotency + reconciliation wiring $30K–$120K/partner
Quest design + workflow buildout Durable workflow graphs, gate/consent design, visual-builder templates $25K–$150K
SurrealDB provisioning + tuning Schema, G1 durability config, optional S3-compatible bucket offload $15K–$40K
Identity federation buildout IdP integration + SSI Verified Credentials wiring + NEOS identity bridging $50K–$200K
Federation gateway deployment (Large only) Multi-tenant gateway + cross-org node federation setup $200K–$800K one-time
30-day hypercare Stabilization + rail tuning Included

PrimusNeo retains the right to harden, generalize, and redistribute any non-bespoke adapter through the AZOA adapter library (a Y2+ revenue tailwind for both the AZOA project and PrimusNeo).

5. Sustained upkeep + infra

Annual sustained upkeep = 20–25% of license (above the all-layer 18% floor because rails need active maintenance against upstream chain and partner API changes).

Infra (pass-through + 12% mgmt fee):

  • Managed node / federation gateway hosting (regional or multi-region): $2,000–$15,000/mo by tier
  • API gateway + observability: $500–$5,000/mo by call volume
  • Chain gas reserve: highly variable by chain + usage; quoted per engagement
  • SurrealDB storage (RocksDB-backed, G1 durability): sized per node; optional S3-compatible bucket offload at $0.012–$0.025/GB-month — SurrealDB is the sole data engine; there are no other storage backends to operate
  • Identity provider pass-through: at provider rates (Okta/Entra/Auth0 seat costs flow through)

6. Bundle interactions

AZOA is the value-motion seam that makes every other bundle composition viable. It is almost never sold alone above the Small tier — buyers who pick it always end up wanting the layers it federates.

Other layer What you unlock together
+ NEOS Governance decisions become quest gates: councils authorize, AZOA executes and settles, and the signed decision trail flows across orgs. Required duo for any multi-org or regulated engagement.
+ Fractal Engine Quest telemetry and settlement history become first-class signals; Fractal reads the workflow ledger into auditable intelligence. Common duo for finance regulatory reporting, manufacturing IT/OT, healthcare settlement.
+ AMP External system state and value events surface into the AMP spatial scene (settlement milestones become asset-attached, alerts become room-attached). Required for any operational-twin engagement at scale.
Full Stack AZOA as the connective settlement tissue is what makes the federated stack actually federated.

7. Two worked engagement examples

7.1 Mid AZOA-only: 4-plant mid-cap manufacturer wanting an IT/OT bridge (Tier-2 Mid)

  • Buyer: CIO + VP Operations at a $400M revenue mid-cap discrete manufacturer with 4 plants in NA. Existing stack: SAP S/4HANA, Rockwell FactoryTalk MES, GE Proficy historian, mixed PLC fleet
  • Scope: AZOA as the IT/OT integration and workflow fabric, replacing 3 brittle in-house Python ETL pipelines and 1 legacy MuleSoft instance the team wants to retire; supplier-payment quests gated on verified receipt events
  • Quote:
  • AZOA Mid: $130K/yr (15 rails/integrations: 4 plants × 3 systems + 3 cross-plant)
  • No bundle (single-layer)
  • Y1 services (~2.5x license): $325K (connector buildouts: SAP $120K + Rockwell $180K + Proficy $80K, with bundle discounts)
  • Y1 upkeep (Silver SLA, 22%): starts Y2 at $29K
  • Infra: $4K/mo
  • Y1 total: ~$500K · 3-yr TCO: ~$1.0M
  • Friction-cost anchor: MuleSoft existing spend $180K/yr + 2 FTE Python engineers maintaining ETL at $300K/yr fully loaded + integration-debt-driven IT/OT incidents costing ~$400K/yr in downtime → $880K/yr direct friction
  • Capture rate: $500K ÷ $880K = 57% capture of direct friction in Y1 (high because AZOA is genuinely displacing line items, not just adding value)
  • Y2 expansion path: Add Fractal Engine for IT/OT signal intelligence (a likely $200K/yr addition)

7.2 Large AZOA+NEOS: National port-operator network (Tier-3 Large)

  • Buyer: CSCO + CIO at a national port operator running 8 terminals + coordination with 5 ocean carriers, 3 rail carriers, customs authority
  • Scope: AZOA as the cross-party data-and-settlement fabric (Navis N4 connectors, customs single-window integration, carrier APIs, IoT in-transit visibility ingest, milestone-gated settlement quests); NEOS as the governance-neutral consortium fabric that explicitly solves the TradeLens-shaped failure mode
  • Quote:
  • AZOA Large: $720K/yr (50+ rails/integrations, multi-org federation gateway on)
  • NEOS Large: $220K/yr (cross-org councils for SLA + data-sharing + dispute resolution)
  • Duet bundle: -10% → $846K/yr license
  • Y1 services (~2.2x blend): $1.86M
  • Y1 upkeep (Gold SLA, 25%): $212K (starts Y2)
  • Infra: $250K–$600K/yr
  • Y1 total: ~$3.0M–$3.5M · 3-yr TCO: ~$6.5M–$8M
  • Friction-cost anchor: TradeLens precedent: $1B+ collectively spent and lost across the prior consortium attempt. Per the logistics research brief, project44/FourKites $300K+/yr standing visibility cost displaced or augmented. EU CBAM compliance starting Jan 2026 adds $500K–$2M/yr at this scale. Combined friction window: $3M–$8M/yr
  • Capture rate: $846K ÷ $5M = ~17% value share — defensible inside the 10–20% logistics capture band

8. Steward economics

Per ../02-licensing-model.md §4:

  • AZOA steward royalty: 15% of AZOA line-item license revenue (managed-tier), paid quarterly
  • The AZOA project also leads rail-adapter development for certified settlement partners; per-adapter services revenue is often delivered by their authors
  • Adapter reuse (where PrimusNeo hardens and redistributes a non-bespoke adapter) accrues royalty to the AZOA steward on the redistributed license revenue, not the original engagement

9. Risks specific to AZOA

Risk Mitigation
TradeLens-shaped consortium failure NEOS governance-neutrality (no single party owns the network) is the explicit answer. Charter every multi-party deployment with this principle from day 1
MuleSoft / Boomi incumbent displacement resistance Position AZOA as the workflow + settlement fabric the incumbents don’t cover (durable quests, chain + fiat rails, consent-gated identity), not as a pure Web2 iPaaS replacement
Rail maintenance cost cliff Active maintenance baked into 20–25% sustained upkeep; adapter library reuse amortizes cost across customers
Self-host cannibalization of the managed tier The engine is deliberately Apache-2.0 — the funnel. Managed nodes win on ops burden (durability config, guardian sets, reconciliation runbooks are operator responsibilities self-hosters must own), certified rails, and SLAs
Cloud-hyperscaler hosted fork No license defense by design; compete on certified settlement partners, compliance evidence packs, federation gateway, and the PrimusNeo stack integration (Temporal/Grafana precedent: the managed+ecosystem moat holds)
Chain/gas cost volatility Always pass-through; never bundled. Customer signs the gas reserve estimate and gets monthly reconciliation
Real-value routes still gated (RealValueEnabled=false pre-launch safety switch on chain-value routes) Scope early engagements to workflows + identity + fiat-seam settlement; chain-value routes activate per the AZOA roadmap gate, and quotes flag this explicitly

Fractal Engine — Intelligence Engagement Model

Layer: 04 — Intelligence / Organizational Memory Steward: Escherbridge License: Creative Commons BY-NC 4.0 (no ShareAlike — derivative models may carry separate training-data licenses) + PrimusNeo Commercial License (PCL) for any commercial use Reference deployment: Tie Town [TX] operational telemetry + Escherbridge corpus Anchors source: ../research/layer-comparables.md

1. What Fractal Engine actually is (in the buyer’s vocabulary)

Fractal Engine is the intelligence and historical-archive layer — the part of the stack that turns raw operational telemetry into durable archives, queryable historical context, and actionable real-time signals.

The differentiator vs Palantir / Snowflake / Databricks / Splunk / Datadog: Fractal Engine treats NEOS governance events and AMP spatial events as first-class signal types. The other intelligence platforms can ingest anything but don’t understand governance and spatial as native concepts. Fractal does — and that is what makes “the contextualized data layer that 70% of Industry 4.0 pilots keep failing to build” actually buildable.

In the language enterprises already know:

  • A manufacturing CIO: Fractal is the OEE + downtime + scrap + energy intelligence layer tied to live spatial events, not month-end reports.
  • A healthcare CDO: Fractal is the population health + RWE + operational analytics layer that doesn’t require ripping out Innovaccer / Health Catalyst / Arcadia.
  • A bank CRO: Fractal is the regulatory + risk intelligence layer that produces the auditable attestation logs MiCA + OCC Letter 1184 + SAB 122 reward.
  • A utility CSO + COO: Fractal is the predictive APM + carbon MRV + Article 6 attestation intelligence layer.
  • A DAO Treasury Council: Fractal is the treasury policy backtesting + delegate-effectiveness + protocol KPI intelligence layer — the “Llama analytics” capability without the Llama retainer.

Fractal is the layer that converts the federated stack into an operating intelligence, not just a federated coordination system.

2. License posture — Creative Commons with Commercial Restrictions

Use License Cost
Personal / academic / research use of platform + models trained on public corpus CC-BY-NC 4.0 Free
Nonprofit community deployment (cooperative, neighborhood, civic group) with no revenue from the deployment CC-BY-NC 4.0 Free
DAO / protocol with treasury ≤ $5M USD-equiv CC-BY-NC 4.0 Free
Small commercial entity meeting all de-minimis thresholds (≤$1M revenue, ≤10 FTE, ≤250 connected users, no resale) CC-BY-NC 4.0 Free
Above any de-minimis threshold PrimusNeo Commercial License (PCL) See tiered pricing below
Hosting Fractal as a paid analytics service PCL required Tiered
Embedding in any commercial product PCL required Tiered
Government / public-sector entity procuring through paid procurement vehicle PCL required Tiered

IP on derivative models: - Models trained on a customer’s data remain the customer’s IP, period. - Models trained on Escherbridge’s public corpus remain Escherbridge IP and are licensed back to the customer under PCL scope. - Models trained on a blend (which is the most common case in practice) are governed by a per-engagement IP schedule attached to the PCL. Default: customer owns the blended weights for their licensed scope; Escherbridge retains rights to the public-corpus-derived components.

This is more permissive than Palantir’s IP regime and more buyer-friendly than the OpenAI / Anthropic “we own the model, you license access” pattern.

3. Pricing tiers (annual commercial license, before services)

Scale Definition Annual License Services Multiplier (Y1) Typical 3-yr TCO
Small <5 data sources, departmental analytics, <1TB managed $30K–$75K/yr 1–2x $140K–$340K
Mid 5–20 data sources, operational telemetry + historical archive, 1–20TB $100K–$350K/yr 1–2x $450K–$1.6M
Large 20+ sources, cross-org intelligence, real-time + archive, >20TB $500K–$2M+/yr 1–2x $2.2M–$9M+

Pricing mechanic options (default in bold):

  • Tiered enterprise license (default): platform + included data-source count + included compute envelope; overages per the table below
  • Per-data-source: $4K–$25K/data-source/yr (good for departmental sales motion)
  • Per-compute-unit (Snowflake-style): $1.50–$3.50/Fractal-credit; useful for buyers used to Snowflake/Databricks mental model
  • Per-research-query (pharma RWE / clinical analytics context): $5–$500/query depending on depth and dataset size
  • PMPM (healthcare population health / payer context): $0.50–$8 PMPM depending on intensity, well below the $15–$200 PMPM industry range
  • Per-tCO2e managed (energy/ag carbon MRV context): bundled into the tiered license; emerging mechanic if customer prefers

Overage mechanics (above tier-included envelope): - Data sources beyond included: $4K–$15K/source/yr - Compute credits beyond envelope: at per-credit rate - TB beyond included: $25/TB/month (parity with Snowflake)

4. Services mix

Fractal services are moderate (1–2x license Y1) — lighter than AZOA/AMP but heavier than NEOS because data pipeline + model training + ontology mapping is real engineering. Typical Mid-tier SOW:

Service Scope Fixed-fee range
Data source discovery + ontology mapping Inventory of buyer’s data sources, ontology design, signal taxonomy $50K–$150K
Data pipeline buildout Per-source ingest + transform + signal generation (AZOA handles transport; Fractal handles semantics) $25K–$100K per source
Model training Per custom model: feature engineering + training + evaluation + deployment $40K–$200K per model
Operational dashboards + analyst onboarding Per-persona dashboards (CIO, COO, CRO, etc.) + Fractal-query training $30K–$100K
NEOS event + AMP spatial event signal integration (the differentiator) Wiring governance + spatial events as first-class signals into Fractal $40K–$120K
30-day hypercare Model tuning, dashboard polish Included

For pharma / regulated FS / energy: add regulatory model validation ($60K–$250K) and audit-trail attestation buildout ($40K–$120K) as separately scoped items.

5. Sustained upkeep + infra

Annual sustained upkeep = 20–25% of license (above the all-layer floor because model retraining cadence is real ongoing work — quarterly minor retraining + annual major retraining is typical).

Infra (pass-through + 12% mgmt fee):

  • Fractal compute + query: aligned with the buyer’s cloud account preferences (BYO cloud preferred; managed-cloud available)
  • Reference: comparable Snowflake credit cost $2–$4; Databricks DBU $0.15–$0.40 — Fractal is positioned at parity with these on raw compute
  • Storage (warm + cold archive): $0.012/GB-mo warm S3-class; $0.004/GB-mo cold Glacier-class
  • Model serving (GPU): $1.10–$3.95/GPU-hr managed (Modal-class); reserved instances 30–50% off
  • Observability (Datadog/equivalent): pass-through at buyer’s existing rates
  • Optional Escherbridge corpus access (where the customer wants Fractal models pretrained on Escherbridge’s public corpus before customer-data fine-tuning): bundled into PCL or per-engagement priced

6. Bundle interactions

Fractal is the value-multiplier layer. It composes with every other layer to turn coordination into insight.

Other layer What you unlock together
+ AMP Spatial events become first-class signals. Predictive maintenance, OEE, capacity forecasting attach to scene positions. Standard duo for any operational-twin engagement.
+ NEOS Governance events become first-class signals. Contribution accounting → workforce intelligence; council decisions → policy-effectiveness analytics; signed attestations → automated regulatory reporting. Required duo for regulated FS, healthcare, energy.
+ AZOA AZOA is the data-in path; Fractal is the intelligence-out layer. Required duo for any analytics-led engagement; Fractal without AZOA is a stranded analytics tool with no data feed.
Full Stack The federated coordination fabric with native intelligence. This is the configuration that beats Palantir on TCO and beats Snowflake/Databricks on operational context.

7. Two worked engagement examples

7.1 Mid Fractal+AZOA: Regional health-system population analytics (Tier-2 Mid)

  • Buyer: CIO + CDO + CCO at a 4-hospital regional health system with ~800 beds and a 200K-life MA payer arm
  • Scope: Fractal for population health + utilization analytics across the 4 hospitals + payer arm; AZOA for Epic FHIR R4 + Cerner FHIR + claims feed + SDoH data ingest; PMPM-priced license
  • Quote:
  • Fractal Mid: $220K/yr (12 data sources, 8 TB managed); priced as PMPM at $1.10 PMPM × 200K lives = $264K/yr (use the higher of tiered or PMPM)
  • AZOA Mid: $140K/yr (10 integrations: Epic + Cerner + 3 claims + 2 SDoH + 3 internal)
  • Duet bundle: -10% → $364K/yr license
  • Y1 services (~1.6x blend): $580K (Epic FHIR connector $200K, Cerner FHIR $180K, ontology mapping $80K, dashboards $90K + others)
  • Y1 upkeep (Gold SLA — healthcare default, 25%): $91K (starts Y2)
  • Infra: $80K–$180K/yr (HITRUST-compliant cloud surcharge applies)
  • Y1 total: ~$1.1M–$1.2M · 3-yr TCO: ~$2.4M–$2.8M
  • Friction-cost anchor: Per the healthcare research brief: interop fabric at this scale runs $5M–$15M over 3 yrs (Redox at $35K/month floor alone is ~$1.3M over 3 yrs). Innovaccer/Arcadia at $600K–$3M/yr. Combined Y1 displacement opportunity: $1.5M–$3M/yr
  • Capture rate: $364K ÷ $2.2M (midpoint) = ~17% value share — defensible inside the 8–20% healthcare capture band
  • HITRUST gate: PrimusNeo HITRUST i1 prerequisite for this RFP (the brief flags 81% of hospitals require it)

7.2 Large Full-Stack: Tier-1 OEM global manufacturing program (Tier-3 Large)

This is the manufacturing example from amp.md §7.2 extended into a full-stack engagement. See that file for the AMP-side numbers; here we focus on Fractal’s contribution:

  • Fractal Large in the bundle: $800K/yr (20+ data sources across 12 plants: PLC + MES + historian + ERP + quality + energy + workforce)
  • Custom Fractal models: predictive OEE per line, downtime root-cause classifier, scrap classifier, Scope 3 emissions estimator for DPP — typically 4–8 custom models in Y1
  • Y1 Fractal services: $1.2M (significant chunk of the overall $2.8M services number)
  • Y2+ steady state: Fractal upkeep + quarterly model retraining ~$200K/yr
  • Value contribution: Fractal is the layer that converts the spatial twin from “live data viewer” into “operating intelligence” — the difference between OEE + downtime + scrap + Scope 3 being visible and being predicted. The 5–15% manufacturing capture band assumes Fractal is part of the stack; without it the capture rate drops to 1–3%.

8. Steward economics

Per ../02-licensing-model.md §4:

  • Escherbridge royalty: 18% of Fractal Engine line-item license revenue, paid quarterly
  • Escherbridge is the primary delivery partner for Fractal ML engineering, custom model development, and ontology design; services revenue accrues to Escherbridge directly when they deliver
  • The CC-NC + PCL structure preserves Escherbridge’s stewardship from cloud-hyperscaler appropriation of the Fractal codebase
  • Escherbridge’s public corpus is a strategic asset shared across the federation; access terms are governed by the per-engagement IP schedule

9. Risks specific to Fractal Engine

Risk Mitigation
Comparison to Palantir (price + capability) We are explicitly below Palantir commercial ($250K–$500K/yr entry vs our $100K Mid start) and beat them on operational ontology native support. Lead with TCO comparison.
Comparison to Snowflake/Databricks (commodity perception) We sit above Snowflake/Databricks raw compute in price but bring native NEOS + AMP signal types and the operational ontology layer they don’t have. Position as the operating-intelligence layer that uses a data warehouse, not as a data warehouse

Agriculture — Engagement Model

Vertical: Agriculture (row crop, specialty, livestock, cooperatives, ag inputs, regenerative ag, MRV) Research brief: ../research/agriculture.md Anchors: ../01-pricing-model.md, ../research/layer-comparables.md

1. The friction-cost anchor

Agriculture’s friction is distributed across three distinct windows — compliance overhead, coordination inefficiency, and broken data infrastructure — and they compound on one another at every tier.

FSMA compliance tax (small/mid scale): USDA data shows farms with $25K–$250K annual produce sales spend up to 60% of profits on FSMA compliance. A $150K-revenue specialty crop farm carrying $90K in compliance overhead is a buyer already paying enterprise-software money for paper-and-labor solutions. That’s the anchor.

Per-acre software density: FieldView has established $4–$10/acre/yr as the mass-market baseline for FMIS. CropX (hardware + SaaS) reaches $15–$25/acre/yr. At 5,000 acres, FieldView ceiling = $50K/yr. At 100,000 acres (mid regional co-op), the comparable full-suite spend is $400K–$2M/yr blended. The per-acre market has priced the buyer; PrimusNeo captures a portion of that existing budget while displacing compliance and MRV spend they’re currently absorbing separately.

MRV cost collapse: Traditional soil carbon verification runs $50–$100/acre. Boomitra’s satellite-ML approach claims below $10/acre at continental scale — a 5–10x reduction. The voluntary ag carbon market itself contracted 57% in 2024 ($84.9M → $36.1M) driven by methodology disputes and quality concerns, with Nori’s September 2024 shutdown as the visible failure event. The lesson is not that MRV is dead; it’s that MRV platforms that promised returns without verifiable outcome evidence failed. The Article 6.4 UN carbon market launching 2025–2026 with UN-endorsed credits expected at premium pricing vs voluntary is the re-entry event that makes this investable again — but only for platforms that can produce third-party-verifiable attestations.

EUDR compliance cost: For large operators with EU-market soy, cattle, cocoa, coffee, palm oil, rubber, or wood exposure, plot-level geolocation compliance is mandatory by December 30, 2026. EUDR upstream costs — physical segregation, traceability infrastructure, geolocation data — remain substantial even after EC simplification measures, and can exceed thin ag commodity margins in high-risk origins (Mongabay, Feb 2026). For a large trader handling 500K+ acres of soy supply chain, this is a $2M–$10M/yr compliance program waiting to happen.

Computed friction windows by scale:

Scale Representative buyer Primary friction Annualized friction window
Small 5K-acre family farm FSMA + ad-hoc tools $40K–$120K/yr
Mid 50K-acre regional co-op ERP interop + MRV enrollment + member data governance $400K–$2M/yr
Large National co-op federation / ag input giant EUDR compliance + Scope 3 + multi-system interop $5M–$25M/yr

PrimusNeo capture band for agriculture: 8–18% of compliance + MRV spend (per the pricing model vertical table). The per-acre software budget is the comparison anchor; the compliance and MRV spend is the friction being displaced.

2. PrimusNeo positioning per layer in agriculture

AMP — Field and livestock spatial twin (PLAN Systems)

AMP is the authoritative field-and-facility spatial record: field boundaries, soil zones, irrigation infrastructure, livestock areas, storage facilities, and agronomic event log. In the ag buyer’s vocabulary: the offline-capable operational ground layer that ties together precision guidance, sensor telemetry, satellite imagery, and field event records into one navigable scene.

Critical requirement: offline-first. Rural connectivity is a primary disqualifier for cloud-only platforms across large portions of the US Corn Belt, Great Plains, and international growing regions. AMP’s offline-first architecture (the PLAN core runtime operates without continuous cloud connectivity) is not a feature — it is a prerequisite for any farm-edge deployment. This is the explicit point of failure for every Web-native spatial platform (Spatial.io, Microsoft Mesh) and many FMIS competitors.

AMP prices per farm site at $4K–$30K/site/yr for production deployments. For a cooperative serving 200 member farms, cooperative-friendly flat-rate site licensing at $500–$3,000/site/yr is the right mechanic — it avoids per-acre metering complexity across variable field sizes and aligns with how co-op boards think about distributing costs to members.

NEOS — Cooperative governance and member-data sovereignty (OmniOne)

Farmer data sovereignty is the primary adoption barrier in precision agriculture. The GAO January 2024 precision agriculture report formally identified farm data sharing and ownership as the top adoption challenge. Civil Eats (March 2026) described farm data as “a hidden crop for corporate tech.” Only 36% of smaller farms planned AI adoption vs 81% of large farms — the trust gap is structural and will not be resolved by privacy policy updates alone.

NEOS addresses this at the protocol layer. For a cooperative deployment, NEOS instantiates member councils where member-elected board governance controls data-sharing policies, contribution accounting tracks which member data contributed to which outputs, and signed on-chain attestation creates a verifiable, auditable record that data was not resold. The CC-NC dual-license structure is itself a signal: the governance layer ships under Creative Commons NonCommercial terms, which signals non-extractive intent to the exact audience (cooperatives, small farms, agronomists) that has been burned by extractive tech before.

For national cooperative federations, NEOS enables holonic federation — local co-op councils retain local sovereignty while participating in federation-level governance without surrendering member data control to the national entity.

AZOA — ERP interop across cooperative member systems (AZOA)

Co-op member systems are heterogeneous by design. A regional grain co-op’s technology estate typically includes AgVend (retail omnichannel), SSI cooperative software, Agris (farm accounting), one or more FMIS platforms across members, a grain-ticketing system, and commodity price feeds. AZOA is the integration layer that connects these without requiring rip-and-replace of any incumbent — the same positioning as MuleSoft for Web2 enterprise integration, extended to the ag-specific system landscape that MuleSoft’s standard connector library doesn’t cover.

AZOA also carries the carbon registry connectors (Verra VCS, Gold Standard, Article 6.4 national registries) that are required for MRV credit issuance. These are purpose-built AZOA connectors delivered by AZOA.

Fractal Engine — Yield prediction, carbon attribution, MRV signal generation (Escherbridge)

Fractal Engine is the intelligence layer that turns AMP field events, NEOS governance logs, and AZOA-ingested system data into defensible analytical outputs: yield prediction models, input-cost optimization recommendations, Scope 3 carbon attribution per field/per commodity lot, and MRV signal generation for carbon credit verification.

The Escherbridge precedent in carbon attribution is relevant here: Indigo Ag’s sale of 2.85M tCO2e to Microsoft represents the largest single soil carbon transaction to date, built on satellite + agronomic intelligence at scale. Fractal’s analytics depth, tuned to Escherbridge’s corpus, is the infrastructure that enables this class of transaction — and the explicit failure mode we cite when asked why PrimusNeo is different from Nori.

Fractal is also where EUDR plot-level geolocation compliance outputs are produced: satellite-verified field boundaries + crop identification + deforestation-risk scores, formatted for EU Due Diligence Statement submissions.

3. Engagement tiers — Small / Mid / Large

Tier 1 — Small: Family farm or small cooperative (<5,000 acres, <$10M revenue)

Bundle: AMP (field spatial twin, offline-capable) + Fractal Engine (yield prediction + input optimization). NEOS and AZOA added if co-op-connected; otherwise lightweight. Cooperative-friendly site licensing.

Worked example — Small Specialty Crop Operation (Tier 1)

  • Buyer: 12-member vegetable cooperative, 800 acres in the Rio Grande Valley. FSMA Produce Safety Rule compliance is costing $60K/yr in record-keeping labor + consultant fees. Considering a specialty carbon program.
  • Scope: AMP for field boundaries + agronomic event log (FSMA audit trail); Fractal for input-cost modeling + soil health indexing; NEOS (CC-NC, free — cooperative with <$1M revenue per member, non-commercial deployment) for member governance.
  • Quote:
  • AMP Small: $22K/yr (per-site, 3 field clusters, offline-first mode required)
  • Fractal Small: $35K/yr (4 data sources: soil tests, weather, irrigation sensors, yield history)
  • Duet bundle: -10% → $51K/yr license
  • Y1 services (Atom deployment SKU, $55K + basic field data migration, $20K): $75K
  • Y1 upkeep (Bronze SLA, 18%): $9K/yr (starts Y2)
  • Infra (edge node + cloud reference small): ~$800/mo
  • Y1 total: ~$136K · 3-yr TCO: ~$225K
  • Friction-cost anchor: $60K/yr FSMA compliance labor + $15K/yr ad-hoc satellite imagery + $20K/yr failed carbon program enrollment overhead = $95K/yr. Capture rate: $51K ÷ $95K = 54% of direct friction in steady state — but the FSMA audit trail alone ($60K displaced) justifies the AMP license independently.
  • Harvest-contingent payment terms: offered on Y1 license; 50% due at spring deployment, 50% due post-harvest.

3-yr TCO range (Tier 1 Small): $60K–$150K

Tier 2 — Mid: Regional cooperative or mid-scale corporate farm (5K–100K acres, $10–$200M revenue)

Bundle: AMP + AZOA + NEOS + Fractal Engine (full stack, cooperative-optimized).

Worked example A — Regional Grain Cooperative (Tier 2, Co-op)

  • Buyer: 400-member grain cooperative in Iowa, 80,000 acres across members, $120M annual revenue. Technology estate: AgVend retail platform, SSI cooperative ERP, mixed FMIS across members. Scope 3 reporting required by Walmart grain sourcing program. EUDR soy exposure for EU export customers.
  • Scope: AMP for member field spatial twin (offline-first for farm-edge members) + cooperative facility layer; AZOA connectors to AgVend + SSI + 3 FMIS platforms (Conservis, Traction Ag, John Deere Operations Center); NEOS for cooperative board governance + member-data sovereignty assurance + on-chain attestation that member data was not resold; Fractal for Scope 3 attribution per grain lot + EUDR plot-level geolocation + yield prediction.
  • Quote:
  • AMP Mid: $140K/yr (12 cooperative facilities + member farm aggregate spatial layer)
  • AZOA Mid: $120K/yr (15 integrations: AgVend + SSI + 3 FMIS + 3 grain ticketing + carbon registry + 7 others; per-connector + base)
  • NEOS Mid: $60K/yr (5 councils: board + 4 regional member chapters)
  • Fractal Mid: $180K/yr (12 data sources: member yield histories, soil tests, weather stations, grain tickets, Scope 3 + EUDR model)
  • Full-stack bundle: -25% → $375K/yr license
  • Y1 services (~1.8x license blend, ag-weighted): $675K (Hex deployment SKU $550K + AgVend connector $80K + SSI connector $60K + FMIS connectors $90K, with bundle discounts; governance design workshop $45K)
  • Y1 upkeep (Silver SLA, 22%): $83K/yr (starts Y2)
  • Infra (mid cloud reference + edge nodes at 4 elevator sites): $8K/mo
  • Y1 total: ~$1.15M · 3-yr TCO: ~$2.8M
  • Friction-cost anchor: $180K/yr current Scope 3 reporting consultant + $120K/yr EUDR compliance work begun + $200K/yr siloed FMIS subscriptions across members + $150K/yr integration maintenance = $650K/yr. Capture rate: $375K ÷ $650K = 58% of direct friction in Y2 steady state.

Worked example B — Mid-Scale Corporate Farm (Tier 2, Corporate)

  • Buyer: Corporate row-crop operation, 40,000 acres across 6 farm units in the Midwest, $80M revenue. Lead agronomist controls field data access and vendor trial permissions. ESG lead requires Scope 3 reporting for parent company’s annual report.
  • Scope: AMP for farm-unit spatial twins (no cooperative governance needed); AZOA for ERP connector (NetSuite) + 2 FMIS platforms + soil sensor network ingest; Fractal for yield prediction + Scope 3 attribution + input-cost optimization models. NEOS lightweight (internal governance only, Bronze SLA).
  • Quote:
  • AMP Mid: $95K/yr (6 farm units, per-site)
  • AZOA Small (high end): $55K/yr (8 integrations)
  • Fractal Mid: $140K/yr (8 data sources)
  • Triad bundle: -18% → $237K/yr license
  • Y1 services (~2.0x blend): $474K
  • Y1 upkeep (Silver SLA, 22%): $52K/yr (starts Y2)
  • Infra: $5K/mo
  • Y1 total: ~$771K · 3-yr TCO: ~$1.9M
  • Friction-cost anchor: $220K/yr siloed technology spend + $180K/yr Scope 3 consultant + $80K/yr MRV program overhead = $480K/yr. Capture rate: $237K ÷ $480K = 49% of direct friction.

3-yr TCO range (Tier 2 Mid): $1.2M–$6M

Tier 3 — Large: Ag input company, global trader, or national cooperative federation ($200M+ revenue)

Bundle: Full stack — all four layers — with multi-region rollout, cross-commodity intelligence, and regulatory compliance infrastructure (EUDR, Article 6, FSMA, CBAM).

Worked example — National Cooperative Federation (Tier 3, Co-op)

  • Buyer: National grain cooperative federation with 12 regional member co-ops, 2M aggregate member acres, $3B combined revenue. Equivalent profile: CHS or GROWMARK federated entity. Technology: Cooperative Ventures portfolio FMIS, multiple ERP systems, existing Verra VM0042 carbon program under economic pressure from Dec 2024 fee revision.
  • Scope: AMP federated spatial layer (all 12 regional co-ops, member farm aggregation, offline-first at farm edge); AZOA federation engine (50+ integrations: co-op ERPs, FMIS platforms, grain trading systems, carbon registries including Verra VCS + Article 6.4 national registries, EUDR EU traceability API); NEOS federated governance (12 regional councils + 1 national federation council; member-data sovereignty assurance at every tier; on-chain attestation for all data sharing events); Fractal cross-commodity SC intelligence (Scope 3 per grain lot + soy deforestation risk + carbon credit MRV signal generation + yield intelligence across all regions).
  • Quote:
  • AMP Large: $600K/yr (federation + 12 regional spatial instances)
  • AZOA Large: $800K/yr (50+ integrations, federation engine, cross-registry connectors)
  • NEOS Large: $280K/yr (federated governance, 13 councils, 2M-acre attestation scope)
  • Fractal Large: $900K/yr (20+ sources, cross-commodity intelligence, real-time + archive)
  • Full-stack bundle: -25% → $1.935M/yr license
  • Y1 services (~1.7x license blend, federation-weighted): $3.3M (Tessellation deployment SKU low end $3.5M; blended with existing co-op infrastructure = $3.3M)
  • Y1 upkeep (Gold SLA, 25%): $484K/yr (starts Y2)
  • Infra (large cloud reference, multi-region + 30 edge nodes at elevator sites): $35K/mo
  • Y1 total: ~$5.65M · 3-yr TCO: ~$14.5M
  • Friction-cost anchor: EUDR compliance program $3M–$8M/yr for EU soy/cattle exposure + current VM0042 carbon program admin $1.5M/yr under fee pressure + multi-co-op interop overhead $2M/yr + Scope 3 consultant and reporting $1M/yr = $7.5M–$12.5M/yr. Capture rate: $1.935M ÷ $10M (midpoint) = 19% value share — inside the 8–18% agriculture capture band (toward the high end, justified by EUDR regulatory tailwind and clear displacement of consultant spend).

3-yr TCO range (Tier 3 Large): $15M–$75M

4. Industry-specific procurement signals

Decision-maker map by segment:

Segment Primary decision authority Critical influencer Budget cycle
Cooperative Member-elected board (risk-averse, consensus-driven) General manager + lead agronomist Annual (Oct–Mar board approval)
National co-op federation CEO + CTO/CIO + technology committee Cooperative Ventures portfolio team (CHS/GROWMARK gateway) Annual + capital program cycles
Corporate farm (mid) Ops manager + CFO Lead agronomist — controls field data access and vendor trial permissions Fiscal year-end planning
Ag input company (Bayer, AGCO, CNH) CTO/CIO + divisional president Digital ag product team Quarterly operating review
MRV/carbon program Sustainability director + CFO Third-party verifier relationship Carbon program enrollment windows

The agronomist is the critical influencer. At mid-sized corporate farms and cooperative tech evaluations, the lead agronomist controls field data access and vendor trial permissions. No pilot proceeds without agronomist buy-in. Motions that bypass the agronomist and go straight to the CFO reliably stall in technical evaluation. The selling motion must engage the agronomist first, produce a one-season pilot with measurable yield or input-cost data, then use that evidence for board or executive escalation.

Sales cycle timing:

  • Co-op enterprise software: 12–24 months board-approval cycle
  • MRV/carbon program enrollment at scale: 18–36 months
  • Corporate farm technology adoption: 9–18 months

Planting-cycle awareness is non-negotiable. Technology decisions in North American grain farming are made October through February for spring deployment. A pilot that starts in March cannot generate board-credible ROI evidence until the following harvest (October–November at earliest). The sales calendar must align to this rhythm: discovery and scoping in summer, proposal and board presentation in fall, contract signature by January, deployment by planting season.

Buying triggers that accelerate the cycle:

  • EUDR compliance deadline pressure (December 30, 2026 for large operators)
  • Walmart/Unilever/Nestlé Scope 3 supplier requirements with contractual deadlines
  • USDA EQIP/CSP conservation program eligibility linked to digital record-keeping
  • Article 6.4 carbon market launch creating premium credit pricing window
  • Co-op member demand for input cost transparency (particularly fertilizer price tracking in CBAM-adjacent supply chains)

Cooperative Ventures (CHS + GROWMARK) is the gateway to the cooperative segment. Both organizations route technology investment through their $50M Cooperative Ventures fund. Vendor relationships typically begin with the Cooperative Ventures portfolio team before reaching operational buyers. A fund relationship is a prioritized channel entry point.

5. Rejection-risk mitigations

Data sovereignty — PRIMARY BARRIER

The risk: Farmers fear that precision data (field boundaries, yield maps, soil tests, application records) shared with large-platform vendors becomes competitive intelligence for input companies or grain traders. This is not irrational; multiple documented instances of data being shared with parent company commodity desks have been reported (Civil Eats, March 2026). Only 36% of smaller farms planned AI adoption vs 81% of large farms — the trust gap is structural.

The mitigation stack: 1. Transparent data ownership terms in the PFEL: customer data is exportable in machine-readable form at any time, non-negotiable. 2. Farmer-owned data by contract: no resale, no use beyond licensed scope, no sharing with parent entity (Bayer, Corteva) without explicit opt-in. 3. On-chain attestation via NEOS: every data-sharing event is signed and anchored on-chain, creating a verifiable record that data was not resold. This is the protocol-level sovereignty guarantee that contractual terms alone cannot provide. 4. CC-NC dual-license signal: NEOS ships under Creative Commons NonCommercial terms. The license itself communicates non-extractive intent to the cooperative and smallholder audience. 5. Member council governance: NEOS cooperative councils give farmer-members visibility and control over data governance policy — not just a vendor promise.

Rural connectivity

AMP’s offline-first architecture is the mitigation. Intermittent-sync mode means field operations continue without cloud connectivity; synchronization occurs when connectivity is available (at the elevator, at the farm office, at the co-op facility). This is a hard architectural requirement; it must be demonstrated in the pilot, not promised in a slide deck.

ROI per acre too thin

At $200–$500/acre commodity crop margins, a $10–$15/acre software stack requires demonstrable 2–5% yield lift or input cost reduction just to break even. The mitigation is a Fractal Engine-powered quantified yield-lift model: Fractal establishes a pre-deployment baseline in the instrumentation phase (60 days), then tracks yield, input spend, and variable cost per acre against the baseline across one full growing season. The model produces a per-acre ROI number the agronomist can present to the board. This is not a case study — it is a buyer-specific measurement against buyer-specific baseline data.

Broken farm-tech promises

Nori (shutdown September 2024), multiple failed carbon programs, John Deere data lock-in controversies, and Bayer’s years of financial losses on Climate Corporation have created deep market skepticism. The mitigation is verifiable third-party outcome evidence — not vendor case studies. Cite the Nori collapse explicitly as the failure mode PrimusNeo is designed to avoid: a platform that promised MRV at scale without verifiable attestation infrastructure collapsed when buyer scrutiny increased. Fractal + NEOS produce verifiable, on-chain-attested MRV outputs that a third-party verifier can independently confirm.

Seasonal cash flow

Farm operations are highly seasonal with cash concentrated post-harvest. Annual subscription billing misaligned to farm cash flow creates real friction. Standard mitigation: installment billing (50% at spring deployment / 50% post-harvest) or harvest-contingent payment terms. This materially improves conversion with small and mid operators. Large co-op and corporate farm buyers are less sensitive to this but appreciate the option as a signal that PrimusNeo understands the agricultural cash cycle.

6. Regulatory and compliance considerations

EU Deforestation Regulation (EUDR) Covers soy, cattle, cocoa, coffee, palm oil, rubber, and wood. Large operators: compliance mandatory by December 30, 2026. Micro and small operators: June 30, 2027. Compliance requires plot-level geolocation for all covered commodities entering the EU market, plus due diligence documentation and a risk assessment system. For any US grain co-op or ag trader with EU soy, cattle, or commodity exposure, this is a hard deadline creating a procurement event. AZOA connectors to the EU EUDR traceability system and Fractal-generated deforestation risk scores per field directly satisfy the technical compliance requirement.

Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM) Nitrogen fertilizer is in CBAM scope. Direct commodity crops are not yet included but remain under ongoing review; inclusion would fundamentally reshape ag supply chain economics. The indirect input-cost pressure from CBAM on nitrogen fertilizer creates demand for Fractal-powered fertilizer cost optimization and Scope 3 attribution models.

USDA NIFA and FSMA The FSMA Produce Safety Rule’s record-keeping, traceability, and food safety plan requirements are a direct buying trigger for AMP (field event log = FSMA audit trail) and AZOA (traceability connectors to FDA systems). The USDA NIFA Food Safety Outreach Program provides technical assistance funding to specialty crop producers — a grant funding pathway for small-tier engagements.

Article 6 / Paris Agreement COP29 (November 2024) finalized remaining technical guidance. As of March 2025, 97 bilateral agreements across 59 countries are active. Article 6.4 UN carbon market launch expected 2025–2026. UN-endorsed credits are expected at premium pricing vs voluntary market credits — potentially re-energizing soil carbon MRV investment after the 2024 contraction. Fractal’s MRV signal generation + NEOS on-chain attestation directly produces outputs that qualify for UN-endorsed credit issuance under Article 6.4 methodology, rather than the weakened voluntary market standards that drove the 2024 collapse.

Verra VM0042 and Gold Standard VM0042 v2.0 (active May 2023) is the primary compliance pathway for row-crop carbon. The December 2024 Verra fee revision ($5,000 upfront per verification review + per-credit issuance fees) squeezes developer economics at low credit prices — which is why Fractal’s Boomitra-class cost reduction (below $10/acre at scale vs $50–$100/acre traditional soil sampling) is the economic enabling factor, not a nice-to-have. Gold Standard co-benefit documentation requirements add cost but serve premium buyer segments willing to pay above-market for co-benefit-verified credits.

NEOS + Fractal attestation path: A NEOS-attested governance event (the farmer’s data-sharing consent and program enrollment) combined with Fractal’s satellite-ML MRV signal generates the complete evidentiary package for both Article 6.4 submission and Verra VM0042 issuance. This is the two-layer combination that directly satisfies both regulatory regimes.

  • AGCO Digital Solutions ($150–$300/hr dealer-delivered): AGCO’s dealer channel is embedded with mid-scale corporate farm operators who already have AGCO equipment relationships. Natural mid-tier co-sell for AMP + Fractal deployments in AGCO-fleet operations.
  • ADM Outcomes ($500K–$3M per engagement): Multi-year regenerative ag transition and Scope 3 advisory engagements. ADM Outcomes is the implementation partner for large-scale carbon and sustainability programs — a natural steward for MRV-heavy Fractal deployments.

Traction Ag’s $10M Series A (May 2024) led by Cooperative Ventures signals which FMIS platforms are on the co-op shortlist — and demonstrates that the fund team is actively influencing technology decisions across both organizations’ operational buyer networks. A fund-level relationship unlocks the cooperative segment more reliably than any direct-to-board sales motion.

Construction — Engagement Model

Vertical: Construction & AEC (GC/Sub, Real Estate Developers, Infrastructure, MEP, Smart Buildings / FM) Research brief: ../research/construction.md Anchors: ../01-pricing-model.md, ../research/layer-comparables.md

1. The friction-cost anchor

Construction/AEC is a $16T global market running on fragmented, often-incompatible multi-vendor software stacks — yet owner digital program budgets reliably run at 1–2% of total capex (McKinsey), and advisory retainers from AECOM or Arup on mega-projects consume an additional 0.2–0.5% of capex in fees alone. Those numbers set the ceiling from which PrimusNeo carves its 5–15% capture band.

Friction windows by customer size:

Segment Capex / Revenue basis Digital program budget (1–2% capex or ACV proxy) Typical friction window
Small GC/Sub (<$50M revenue, 1–2 projects) $10M–$50M ACV $100K–$1M/yr $40K–$90K 3-yr TCO (Procore entry + ACC + Fieldwire)
Mid regional contractor/developer ($50–500M, 5–30 projects) $50M–$500M ACV $500K–$5M/yr $350K–$800K 3-yr TCO (Procore + ACC BIM + CDE + DroneDeploy + One Click LCA)
Large ENR Top-100 / mega-project ($1B+ capex, national portfolio) $1B–$10B+ capex $10M–$100M/yr $3M–$12M 3-yr TCO (Procore enterprise + Bentley iTwin + Willow Twin + advisory)

The market’s dominant pricing mechanics make the friction cost concrete: Procore’s ACV model runs 0.1–0.3% of Annual Construction Volume (a 25-project regional GC all-in $40K–$120K/yr); Autodesk Build at $1,625/seat/yr for field users; Bentley iTwin enterprise at $200K–$1M+/yr for large infrastructure; Willow Twin at $50K–$250K/yr per campus for smart building FM. One Click LCA Power Pack at $80K–$120K/yr for embodied carbon. These are the incumbent line items PrimusNeo displaces or federates — not by replacing them outright, but by absorbing the interoperability tax they generate among themselves.

A mid-tier GC running Procore + ACC BIM Collaborate (30 seats) + Asite CDE + DroneDeploy Advanced pays approximately $120K–$200K/yr in platform licenses alone before any SI advisory, any duplicate data-entry labor, or any rework attributable to model/field data drift. PrimusNeo’s friction anchor is the gap between what those platforms cost in isolation and what a federated, single-source-of-truth stack would cost — conservatively $250K–$500K/yr in direct and indirect friction at the mid tier.

2. PrimusNeo positioning per layer in construction

AMP is the killer layer in construction. The fundamental coordination problem in every AEC project is that design-side BIM (Autodesk Revit/ACC, Bentley MicroStation) and field-side project management (Procore, Trimble Connect) and FM-side building operations (IBM TRIRIGA, Planon, Honeywell Forge) each maintain their own spatial record — and they never stay synchronized. The design model diverges from the as-built the moment the first RFI is issued. The as-built diverges from the FM model the moment handover documents go into a SharePoint folder no one maintains.

AMP’s federated spatial record — built on the open Hexon file standard, offline-capable, multi-org — is the construction-native answer to this problem. It bridges Autodesk BIM (ACC model ingestion via IFC/RVT), Trimble field data, and Bentley infrastructure models without forcing single-vendor lock-in at any layer. The as-built lives in AMP as an AMP scene. The FM team queries it through their IWMS. The GC’s VDC manager pushes model updates via IFC. No single platform owns the record.

AZOA is the required companion. The construction stack is notoriously siloed. Procore does not natively talk to ACC. ACC does not write back to Trimble Connect without manual export. Asite CDE is separate from both. AZOA certified bidirectional connectors for Procore (webhook-based), ACC (IFC/RVT model ingest + write-back), Bentley iTwin, and Trimble Connect collapse the integration tax that currently costs GC IT teams 1–2 FTE in perpetual connector maintenance. At the small and mid tier, AMP + AZOA is the standard bundle.

NEOS delivers cross-project governance and change-control flows. At mid-to-large scale, construction’s biggest coordination overhead is the approval bureaucracy: RFIs, submittals, change orders, and management-of-change (MOC) on occupied facilities. NEOS structures these as signed, auditable, replayable events — replacing the email-thread-based approval culture that Procore partially addresses but never fully governs, especially across organizational boundaries (owner / GC / design team / authority having jurisdiction).

Fractal Engine converts project data into portfolio intelligence. At portfolio scale, ENR-100 contractors and real estate developers need answers that no individual project platform provides: RFI velocity trends that predict schedule slippage 6 weeks out; embodied carbon analytics that ingest EPD data per material per project for EPBD / Buy Clean Act compliance; financial closeout benchmarking across the portfolio. Fractal also enables RFI predictive triage — classifying incoming RFIs by complexity and downstream cost impact before a project engineer reads them, routing high-risk items to senior PM attention automatically.

Standard bundle and full-stack triggers:

  • Small GC/Sub: AZOA alone (Procore/ACC connector), or AZOA + AMP entry (as-built record without displacing incumbents)
  • Mid regional contractor/developer: AMP + AZOA (federated spatial + integration duo); add NEOS for change-control
  • Large ENR-100 / mega-project: Full AMP + NEOS + AZOA + Fractal as the federated intelligence and governance layer across the portfolio

3. Engagement tiers — Small / Mid / Large

Small: GC or Subcontractor, <$50M Revenue, 1–2 Active Projects

Buyer profile: A 50-person mechanical/electrical sub or a regional residential GC. Running Procore entry + ACC Docs (5 seats) + Fieldwire Pro (5 seats). Primary pain: duplicate data entry between Procore and ACC; no durable as-built record at project closeout; RFIs managed via email threads.

PrimusNeo bundle: AZOA (Procore + ACC bidirectional connector) + AMP entry (as-built spatial record for 1 project)

PrimusNeo at $43K/yr license occupies 43–66% of the direct friction — but eliminates the duplicate entry FTE and the as-built loss risk, which is where the real ROI lives.

Capture rate: 43K ÷ $82K (midpoint) = ~52% of direct friction displaced. Defensible because AZOA is genuinely removing line items, not adding a layer.

Developer/FM small example: A boutique real estate developer completing a 120-unit multifamily project needing a handover-ready as-built and AZOA connector to their IWMS (IBM TRIRIGA entry). Same bundle, scoped to 1 building. Quote similar; AZOA connector scope shifts from Procore to TRIRIGA integration ($20K fixed fee). AMP as-built persists post-handover as the FM spatial record — positioning the Y2 AMP-to-FM upsell.

Mid: Regional Contractor / Developer, $50–500M Revenue, 5–30 Projects

Buyer profile: A regional GC with 20 active projects across 3 states, running Procore ($45K/yr) + ACC BIM Collaborate (25 seats, $18K/yr) + Asite CDE ($60K/yr) + DroneDeploy Advanced ($8K/yr) + early-stage One Click LCA use. Pain: project data lives in four systems; portfolio reporting is a monthly Excel exercise; change-order approvals route through email with no audit trail; ESG officer cannot produce an embodied carbon number without a consultant.

PrimusNeo license at $238K/yr captures 43–68% of direct friction while eliminating the overhead — overall value share roughly 12–15% of total friction window (per McKinsey 1–2% capex digital budget at this revenue scale, the window is $500K–$5M/yr).

Full stack; pricing anchored by per-sf FM spend ($0.10–$0.50/sf/yr at 10M sf managed = $1M–$5M/yr incumbent spend).

4. Industry-specific procurement signals

Primary champions:

  • VDC / BIM Manager at GC or design-build firm: controls day-to-day platform selection for ACC, Procore, and CDE. This is the technical champion who experiences the design/field model drift firsthand and has the authority to run a pilot. Pain: IFC round-trips, duplicate model management, ACC vs. Procore data silos. Entry motion: AZOA Procore + ACC connector eliminates their top-3 complaint.

  • CIO / CDO at ENR-100 contractor: owns enterprise architecture and vendor consolidation mandates. Budget authority $500K+. Receptive to a federated stack argument that reduces the vendor renewal surface without forcing Autodesk or Bentley off the desk. Entry motion: AMP + AZOA as the platform-neutral coordination layer that sits above incumbents.

  • Project Director / Owner’s Rep on capital programs: responsible for schedule, cost, and risk visibility across 10–50 active projects simultaneously. Values federated data without forcing sub-contractors onto a mandated single platform. The AZOA value proposition — “your subs keep using their tool; we connect it bidirectionally” — maps directly to this buyer’s top procurement constraint. Entry motion: AZOA pilot on a single mega-project, funded from the owner’s digital delivery budget.

  • Director of Sustainability / ESG Officer: owns embodied carbon reporting under EU EPBD, CA Buy Clean Act AB 2446, and annual sustainability disclosures. Currently buying One Click LCA or Tally per project, producing disconnected point-in-time snapshots. Needs automated EPD ingestion and real-time compliance monitoring across the portfolio. Entry motion: Fractal with EPD ingest as the ESG automation layer, positioned as eliminating the annual One Click LCA consulting sprint.

RFP trigger events:

  • Owner-mandated ISO 19650 BIM/CDE requirements on public infrastructure tenders (Highways England, GSA, state DOTs, Network Rail). ISO 19650 mandates a structured Common Data Environment — AMP as the ISO 19650-compliant spatial information layer is the direct response. Part 6 (health and safety information management) released in 2025 strengthens the AMP + NEOS dual-layer argument for large publicly funded projects.

  • Post-merger portfolio consolidation: when a PE-backed GC acquires a regional competitor, the CDO inherits two incompatible Procore instances, two CDE contracts, and two model management workflows. AZOA federation (and NEOS for the governance integration) is the de-merger rationalization play.

  • Mega-project mobilization: capital programs above $500M reliably trigger a digital delivery planning phase where the owner mandates a data management standard before the GC mobilizes. This is the highest-urgency entry point — the budget is pre-approved, the timeline is fixed, and the VDC/BIM team is standing by.

  • ESG reporting deadlines: annual sustainability reports, EU SFDR mandatory disclosures for EU real estate funds, SEC climate disclosure rules for US-listed project owners. These create hard calendar deadlines that convert the Fractal pilot conversation into a signed SOW.

5. Rejection-risk mitigations

Paper and email-based culture (sub/trade workforce): A significant portion of the construction workforce — especially subcontractors and trades — operates on paper, WhatsApp, and email. Sub resistance to “yet-another-login” is the top-3 Procore complaint and the primary reason mandated platform adoption fails on GC projects. Mitigation: AZOA connectors are designed to meet the sub at their existing tool of choice (Procore, Fieldwire, email-triggered approvals) rather than forcing onboarding. The AMP spatial record is maintained by the GC’s VDC team; subs contribute via read-only views and email-triggered approval workflows requiring no account creation.

Subcontractor adoption friction: ENR-100 GCs routinely report that sub networks won’t adopt owner-mandated platforms at scale. Any PrimusNeo engagement that depends on sub-side adoption is structurally at risk. Mitigation: scope AZOA to absorb the sub’s existing tool of choice bidirectionally. The sub uses Procore or Fieldwire or email; AZOA federates that data into the GC’s AMP scene and NEOS approval flows without the sub ever touching PrimusNeo directly.

ROI attribution difficulty: Each construction project is unique; there is no clean A/B comparison. CFOs discount vendor-published ROI reports (Procore’s benchmark studies are routinely challenged in procurement). Mitigation: instrument project-level outcomes via Fractal from day one — RFI cycle time (baseline vs. post-implementation), rework dollar value (tied to model/field drift events), and financial closeout speed. Present at the 90-day review with project-level evidence, not portfolio-level benchmarks.

Integration lock-in with Procore and Autodesk: Most ENR contractors are mid-cycle on multi-year Procore and ACC contracts. Adding PrimusNeo requires a clear and provably non-disruptive AZOA story. Mitigation: publish and certify bidirectional Procore webhook integration (Procore’s own marketplace mechanism) and IFC/RVT model ingestion from ACC with write-back. Integration tax = the primary reason pilots stall at POC; eliminating it requires a live reference deployment the champion can demo to procurement, not a spec sheet.

Price sensitivity on thin GC margins (2–4% net): A GC running 3% net on $200M revenue has $6M in profit. A $500K platform engagement is 8% of net profit — a hard conversation with a CFO who has never seen a construction software vendor justify their price at that level. Mitigation: pilot structure with documented-ROI path before portfolio expansion. The Atom or Molecule deployment SKU on a single project at $90K–$320K, with Fractal capturing baseline metrics, produces the CFO evidence needed to sign the portfolio engagement.

6. Regulatory and compliance considerations

EU EPBD (recast 2024) — Digital Building Passports: The recast Energy Performance of Buildings Directive mandates Digital Building Passports — per-building records covering energy performance, materials, and lifecycle data — as part of EU member-state EPBD implementation. Fossil-fuel boiler subsidies ended January 2025. This is an immediate AZOA + AMP demand driver for any EU construction client or EU-listed real estate fund: the Digital Building Passport is exactly the federated spatial + systems record that AMP maintains and AZOA populates from heterogeneous building systems.

California Buy Clean Act / AB 2446: Global Warming Potential limits on structural steel, concrete rebar, flat glass, and insulation on California public works are enforceable as of January 2025. AB 2446 targets a 40% reduction in material carbon intensity by 2035. This drives One Click LCA and EC3 adoption — and creates the Fractal opportunity for automated EPD ingestion and real-time compliance reporting that replaces the annual consulting sprint. Fractal’s embodied carbon model, fed by AZOA-ingested EPD data, is the construction-native answer to this compliance obligation.

UK Net Zero Carbon Buildings Standard (NZCBS) v1 / PAS 2080: NZCBS v1 launched in early 2026, integrating with PAS 2080 and establishing upfront carbon (A1–A5 life-cycle stages) limits per square metre. From 2025, publicly funded projects must be designed without fossil-fuel heating. Fractal’s Scope 3 + embodied carbon analytics, combined with AMP’s as-built spatial record, create the data layer that satisfies PAS 2080 documentation requirements across the design, construction, and operation phases.

ISO 19650 BIM: The global information-management benchmark for the built environment is mandatory in the EU and UK (since 2016) and increasingly required in GCC and India. Part 6, covering health and safety information management, was released in 2025. AMP serves as the ISO 19650-compliant spatial information layer — the structured CDE that the standard requires — while NEOS provides the approval-flow and audit-trail governance that ISO 19650 Part 2 (delivery phase) mandates for CDE workflows.

LEED / BREEAM / Green Star ongoing obligations: Embodied carbon documentation, energy modeling outputs, and operational data submissions tied to credit thresholds create persistent data obligations through building operations. These are not one-time compliance events — they require the live connection between the construction-phase AMP scene and the FM-phase AZOA-connected IWMS that PrimusNeo’s full stack provides.

7. Steward and service partner notes

PLAN Systems (AMP steward): The differentiator in construction is PLAN’s federated spatial record capability across design, field, and FM phases. PLAN delivers AMP spatial environment buildout services — CAD ingest, scene authoring, IFC/RVT model ingest — and is the primary certified implementation partner for AMP on large construction and infrastructure engagements. PLAN royalty: 15% of AMP line-item license revenue.

OmniOne / Green Earth Vision (NEOS steward): Delivers the ETHOS governance design methodology — the charter-authoring and council-structuring work that converts a GC’s change-control email culture into a signed, auditable NEOS governance flow. OmniOne leads governance design workshops and charter authoring across mid-to-large construction engagements. NEOS royalty: 18% of NEOS line-item license revenue.

AZOA (AZOA steward): Authors the certified Procore, ACC (Autodesk Construction Cloud), Bentley iTwin, and Trimble Connect bidirectional connectors that are the primary AZOA value driver in construction. AZOA leads connector development and holds the technical relationship with Procore’s webhook marketplace and Autodesk’s APS (Autodesk Platform Services). AZOA royalty: 15% of AZOA line-item license revenue.

Escherbridge (Fractal Engine steward): Delivers Fractal portfolio analytics, the RFI predictive triage model, and the embodied carbon EPD-ingest pipeline for Buy Clean Act / EPBD compliance. Escherbridge is the primary delivery partner for Fractal ML engineering and the construction-specific ontology. Fractal royalty: 18% of Fractal Engine line-item license revenue.

Energy — Engagement Model

Vertical: Energy (utilities, oil & gas, renewables, grid/ISO, DERs, microgrids, carbon markets) Research brief: ../research/energy.md Anchors: ../01-pricing-model.md, ../research/layer-comparables.md

1. The friction-cost anchor

Energy is PrimusNeo’s highest regulatory tailwind and longest sales cycle simultaneously. Four compounding friction windows define the buyer’s pain and the defensible quote band.

ADMS/DERMS software stack cost: Full-scale Advanced Distribution Management System deployments run $5M–$50M software-only per investor-owned utility, before hardware and SI services (DOE ADMS Advisory). GE Vernova GridOS (ADMS + DERMS) packages for IOUs: $8M–$20M over five-year terms. Schneider EcoStruxure ADMS and Siemens Spectrum Power: comparable at $6M–$15M per IOU. This is the market PrimusNeo enters above — as interoperability and governance fabric, not as displacement.

AVEVA PI System data historian: At scale (1M+ tags for large refineries and grid operators), AVEVA PI System subscription + support runs $500K–$5M/yr. Cognite Data Fusion at supermajor scale: $2M–$8M ARR. AspenTech APM/Mtell entry: $250K–$1M+/yr for mid-size process industry. These historians are not going away; the PrimusNeo entry is to layer governance, interop, and intelligence above them.

NERC CIP compliance burden: CIP-015-1 (Internal Network Security Monitoring) alone carries $13.2M/yr industry-wide across ~1,636 registered entities (Federal Register, September 2024). CIP-013 supply-chain mandates impose structured vendor assessment and attestation workflows across every BES Cyber System. CIP-003-9, effective April 2026, extends supply-chain controls to low-impact BES assets — expanding the addressable compliance burden further. This is direct willingness-to-pay for auditable provenance: NEOS.

Carbon + cross-border compliance overlay: EU CSRD mandatory double-materiality disclosures began FY2024 for large EU-listed energy firms and their non-EU subsidiaries. EU CBAM definitive phase starts January 2026 — requiring certified embedded-carbon data per shipment with financial penalties for non-compliance. Article 6.4 (Paris Agreement ITMOs, finalized at COP29) demands cryptographically auditable provenance chains to prevent double-counting across Nationally Determined Contributions. These three compliance drivers are structural WTP signals for Fractal Engine + NEOS attestation.

Aggregate friction window per engagement tier: Small microgrid/community solar operator: $300K–$800K/yr in incumbent tooling + compliance cost. Mid municipal utility / IPP: $2M–$6M/yr. Large IOU/ISO-RTO/supermajor: $15M–$60M/yr. PrimusNeo’s 5–15% capture rate against these windows is the defensible quote band.

2. PrimusNeo positioning per layer in energy

The critical positioning frame: PrimusNeo enters energy as interoperability and governance fabric above the incumbent ADMS and PI System stacks — not as a displacement play. Attempting to displace GE Vernova, Schneider, or AVEVA triggers the vendor-concentration objection and the OT cyber risk rejection immediately. Positioning above them as the governance, interop, and intelligence fabric sidesteps both while creating genuine additional value.

AMP — Spatial OS (steward: PLAN Systems): AMP’s role in energy is the plant, substation, and grid spatial scene — the operational ground layer for gas turbine sites, offshore platforms, transmission substations, and microgrid nodes. PLAN Systems brings industrial-grade spatial scene authoring for energy assets. Where manufacturing has CAD + MES, energy has P&ID + SCADA + historian; AMP is the layer that ties them into a navigable, governed, offline-capable operational scene. Entry use cases: offshore platform digital twin for APM, substation spatial model for NERC CIP-007 change-management events, and microgrid site visualization for DER operator teams. Tie Town [TX] demonstrates the reference architecture for multi-site energy deployments.

NEOS — Governance (steward: OmniOne): NEOS is the auditable governance fabric for three energy-specific compliance domains. First, NERC CIP-013 supply-chain attestation: NEOS councils structure vendor assessment workflows and produce signed, replayable governance logs that survive a NERC audit. Second, CSRD and CBAM: NEOS provides the governance event log that underlies double-materiality ESG disclosure and embedded-carbon certification per shipment — not just a dashboard, but a signed audit trail. Third, REC and Article 6.4 ITMO provenance: NEOS + Fractal together produce the cryptographically auditable lineage chains that hourly granular REC tracking (EnergyTag) and Article 6.4 ITMOs require to prevent double-counting. OmniOne’s ETHOS methodology for governance design applies directly to the multi-party council structures that cross-utility supply chains and carbon registries require.

AZOA — Interoperability Fabric (steward: AZOA): AZOA is the IT/OT bridge for FERC Order 2222 and the multi-system integration fabric that connects ADMS, SCADA, EMS, DERMS, ETRM, carbon registries, and ISO market interfaces without requiring any incumbent to be displaced. FERC Order 2222 mandates DER market participation down to 100 kW aggregated, requiring interoperable metering, telemetry, and settlement-data flows across heterogeneous utility and aggregator systems — this is AZOA’s exact problem space. AZOA authors the certified DNP3, IEC 61968/70 (CIM), and ICCP connectors that utility architecture review boards require; “open APIs” without certified OT protocol connectors fail at the technical evaluation stage. Carbon registry integrations (M-RETS, PJM-EIS GATS, Article 6.4 registry APIs) are AZOA connector-class integrations. AutoGrid Flex / Uplight DERMS connector: AZOA. ETRM connectivity (Allegro/ION Group, OpenLink): AZOA.

Fractal Engine — Intelligence (steward: Escherbridge): Fractal is the predictive APM, carbon MRV, and REC hourly-granularity intelligence layer. Escherbridge’s analytics depth makes Fractal the highest-value layer for energy operational telemetry: turbine degradation forecasting, transformer health scoring, renewable curtailment prediction, grid stability signal generation. For carbon markets: Fractal provides the MRV (Measurement, Reporting, Verification) intelligence layer that feeds NEOS attestation — from raw telemetry to verified tCO2e, with audit trail attached. For hourly granular RECs and Article 6.4 ITMOs, Fractal’s time-series intelligence resolves the generation and consumption matching at hourly (or sub-hourly) granularity that 24/7 carbon-free energy (CFE) commitments require. Fractal also positions above Watershed ($50K–$500K/yr flat carbon SaaS) and Persefoni ($55K–$250K/yr) as the operational-intelligence layer that produces MRV data rather than simply ingesting spreadsheet exports.

Bundle: AZOA Small + NEOS Small + Fractal Small (carbon MRV entry). AMP optional for microgrid with a physical site. Duet or Triad.
