                            TEFCA QHIN participation, pharmacy, lab, imaging)
Fractal Large:  $900K/yr  (20+ data sources, cross-org population health + RWE, >20TB;
                            PMPM mechanic: $0.75 PMPM × 1.2M attributed lives)
                ──────
Subtotal:       $2.53M/yr
Full-Stack -25%: $1.9M/yr
Y1 services     (~1.8x blend): $3.4M
                  (Epic Cosmos/FHIR primary $300K, 3 Cerner sites $400K,
                   HITRUST-scope connector audit $100K, AZOA federation engine $500K,
                   AMP spatial build-out across 25 sites Lattice SKU $1.5M range,
                   Fractal RWE model training + population models $600K,
                   NEOS consent + IRB governance design OmniOne $200K)
Y1 upkeep       (Platinum SLA, custom): $560K (starts Y2)
Infra           (multi-region, HITRUST-compliant, dedicated K8s): $350K–$700K/yr
                ──────
Y1 TOTAL:       ~$6.2M–$6.6M
3-yr TCO:       ~$13M–$16M

Friction-cost anchor: Epic integration tax at large IDN = $20M–$80M over 3 years (services, connectors, governance overhead). HITRUST r2 at $400K–$800K per 3-year cycle. Innovaccer/Health Catalyst large-system contracts at $2M+/yr. Redox at scale for 50+ integrations: $3M–$6M over 3 years. Combined 3-yr friction: $25M–$90M.

Capture rate: $1.9M/yr ÷ $40M midpoint friction/yr implied = ~5% value share at floor — defensible, with upside as Epic FHIR cert eliminates incremental connector costs for the customer and PMPM scales with lives attributed.

Top-20 pharma variant (RWE focus): Replace AMP with a Fractal Large + AZOA Large + NEOS Mid Triad. Fractal at $1.2M/yr (per-research-query mechanic for RWE; 20+ data sources including EHR network, claims, registry, genomics). 3-yr TCO $8M–$14M vs IQVIA multi-million-dollar annual licenses + Datavant/Aetion RWE SaaS. Capture rate 15–20% of the IQVIA displacement opportunity.

4. Industry-specific procurement signals

Buyer personas and co-buy dynamics

Hospital/Health System:

  • CIO: Primary economic buyer. Priorities: vendor consolidation (fewer point solutions), integration cost reduction, Epic rationalization, HITRUST compliance gate management. Will not move without CCO sign-off on HIPAA posture.
  • CMIO: Clinical safety veto. Any system that touches clinical workflow requires CMIO endorsement. Position PrimusNeo (especially AMP + Fractal) as decision-support, not autonomous clinical action. Pursue Class I/II SaMD pathway proactively.
  • CDO: Data strategy sponsor. Population health mandate drives Fractal + AZOA engagement. AI governance concerns require documented NEOS governance design.
  • CCO / Chief Privacy Officer: HIPAA and HITRUST gating. Will not approve a demo until BAA scope, de-identification methodology, and subprocessor chain are documented. PHI flow documentation must precede all technical conversations.

Payer:

  • CIO + Chief Actuary + VP Population Health. PMPM mechanic aligns naturally with their operating model. VBC and risk-adjustment analytics are primary value drivers.

Pharma / Biotech:

  • VP Data Science, Head of Real-World Evidence, CTO. Budget owners are often outside IT — closer to medical affairs or R&D. Per-research-query pricing resonates; IQVIA displacement is the anchor conversation. IRB and DPIA governance via NEOS is a secondary pitch that accelerates to primary in EU-operating pharma (EHDS + GDPR Article 9 special-category health data).

Procurement mechanics

  • Capital committee: Any IT commitment above $1M requires board or capital committee approval. Build the CFO narrative — friction-cost math, 3-year TCO comparison against Redox + Innovaccer + HITRUST standalone.
  • Decision cycle: 6–18 months. EHR replacement cycles: 5–10 years (do not try to enter on EHR displacement). Analytics and interop refresh cycles: 3–5 years — these are the windows PrimusNeo enters.
  • Budget timing: Hospital fiscal years often run July–June. IT capital budgets are built July–September. Q3–Q4 (Jan–June) is peak signing — deals funded in the prior budget cycle close in Q1–Q2.
  • RFP process: 68% of providers manage procurement manually (2024 HIMSS). RFPs run 6–12 months from issuance to award. Evaluation committees score: technical fit 30–35%, regulatory compliance pass/fail gate, total cost of ownership 25–30%. HITRUST cert attachment is frequently a submission prerequisite — not evaluation criteria but a gate.

5. Rejection-risk mitigations

Risk: Patient safety objection (CMIO/patient safety officer)

Position all Fractal analytics and AMP spatial alerts as decision-support tools for clinicians, not autonomous decision-makers. Pursue Class I or Class II SaMD pathway under FDA guidance early in the engagement. Produce a software as a medical device classification summary before any CMIO technical demo. Do not wait for the objection — preempt it in the discovery conversation.

Risk: HIPAA fear (CCO/Privacy Officer default-reject)

Before any technical demo: (1) deliver a written BAA scope document identifying every PHI touch point; (2) produce de-identification methodology documentation (Safe Harbor or Expert Determination per 45 CFR §164.514); (3) map the complete subprocessor chain including Escherbridge (Fractal), OmniOne (NEOS governance), AZOA (AZOA connectors), and cloud infrastructure providers. Privacy officers who receive this proactively become advocates; those who discover gaps in discovery become blockers.

Risk: “Rip and replace” resistance

The standard hospital objection: “We spent $300M on Epic. We’re not changing it.” Response: “We wrap Epic, we don’t replace it. AMP adds the OR command center intelligence layer Epic doesn’t offer. AZOA compresses your integration spend by 35–60% vs Redox while remaining Epic FHIR R4 certified and App Orchard listed. Fractal adds population health analytics that Epic Cosmos does not provide for your MA payer arm.” Every message must reinforce the augmentation framing.

Risk: Epic/Cerner integration lock-in

Epic’s proprietary API surface (MyChart, Hyperdrive, App Orchard) creates real friction. Epic FHIR R4 certification + App Orchard listing for AZOA is a prerequisite for any large IDN sale — not a differentiator, a requirement. The 6–18 month Epic technical review must begin immediately as a central PrimusNeo investment, not a per-engagement cost. Until App Orchard listing is achieved, large IDN sales require a lighthouse exception path (direct CIO sponsor, waived App Orchard requirement during pilot).

Risk: Procurement cycle mismatch (startup credibility gap)

12–18 month RFP cycles disadvantage early-stage vendors. Lack of healthcare segment references is a hard rejection criterion. The lighthouse program — securing 2–3 segment references (1 community hospital, 1 regional system, 1 pharma or payer) — is the prerequisite for substantive RFP participation. Tie Town serves as the reference architecture proof point but does not substitute for healthcare-specific references. Prioritize lighthouse deals above contract size optimization in the first 18 months.

6. Regulatory + compliance considerations

HIPAA Security Rule (effective May 2026)

The overhaul mandates: MFA for all PHI access, universal PHI encryption at rest and in transit, 24-hour breach notification to HHS (down from 60 days for major breaches), and annual penetration testing. This is not optional and affects every PrimusNeo layer that touches PHI. AZOA connectors carrying PHI must comply by May 2026; AMP scenes with patient location data must comply; Fractal models trained on EHR data must comply. Document the compliance posture per layer in every BAA and customer-facing technical spec.

HITRUST CSF

81% of hospitals and 83% of payers require HITRUST certification from technology vendors before RFP submission. HITRUST i1 (~$70K) is the non-negotiable minimum for any substantive healthcare RFP participation. HITRUST r2 ($100K–$500K, 12–15 month timeline; $400K–$800K per 3-year cycle) is expected for core platforms at large IDNs and payers. PrimusNeo must fund HITRUST i1 as a central investment — not a per-engagement line item. Until i1 is achieved, the healthcare pipeline is restricted to pilot-stage conversations that do not require RFP submission.

ONC TEFCA / QHIN

Common Agreement v2.1 went into effect January 2025; 21,086 organizations are live. QHIN designation takes approximately 12 months. AZOA as TEFCA-native FHIR fabric is a direct differentiator vs Redox (which is not QHIN-native) and vs Health Gorilla (QHIN participant but not a full interop fabric). Pursue QHIN designation for AZOA as a network-level investment.

EU EHDS (Reg EU 2025/327)

In force March 2025; general application March 2027. EHR vendors must certify for interoperability and security by January 2026. Priority health data exchange (patient summaries, ePrescriptions, lab results, medical images, hospital discharge reports, prescriptions) mandatory EU-wide by March 2029. NEOS consent and governance schema is directly revenue-generative for EU-operating health systems, pharma companies, and biotech — DPIA workflow, cross-border data-sharing governance, and patient consent management are all addressable EHDS compliance requirements via NEOS.

EU MDR

If Fractal Engine outputs constitute clinical decision support (Class IIa/IIb SaMD under EU MDR), conformity assessment applies: $20K–$80K per high-risk system. Bundle into the engagement deliverable as a scoped item. Position Fractal as decision-support (not autonomous clinical action) where possible to maintain Class I pathway and minimize MDR overhead.

FDA SaMD (US)

Fractal analytics that inform clinical treatment decisions are subject to FDA digital health guidance. Class I and Class II SaMD (low-to-moderate risk decision support) require classification documentation and potentially a 510(k) or De Novo pathway depending on intended use. Prepare the SaMD classification summary early — before the first CMIO technical demo.

7. Steward + service partner notes

Escherbridge — Fractal Engine steward (RWE and population health specialty)

Escherbridge is the primary delivery partner for all Fractal RWE and population health model development. Their public corpus provides the pretrained baseline for health system deployments; customer EHR + claims + SDoH data fine-tunes the models under the per-engagement IP schedule (customer owns blended weights; Escherbridge retains public-corpus-derived components). Escherbridge earns 18% of Fractal Engine line-item license revenue per the steward royalty schedule. Healthcare is one of their highest-density use cases — route all Fractal healthcare engineering and model-training SOWs through Escherbridge.

OmniOne — NEOS steward (consent and IRB governance design)

OmniOne leads all NEOS governance design using their ETHOS methodology. Healthcare consent management, IRB governance, and EHDS-compliance data-sharing governance are high-value applications of NEOS — OmniOne’s governance design workshops ($35K–$60K per engagement) are the entry point for NEOS in healthcare. OmniOne earns 18% of NEOS line-item license revenue. For EU pharma and health systems navigating EHDS, OmniOne’s DPIA and consent architecture capability is a standalone sellable service that pulls Fractal and AZOA into the engagement.

AZOA — AZOA steward (Epic/Cerner/HIE connector engineering)

AZOA leads connector development for Epic FHIR, Cerner FHIR, Meditech, Athenahealth, and state HIE endpoints. Their connector engineering is the delivery capacity for AZOA healthcare integrations ($120K–$400K per EHR connector). AZOA earns 15% of AZOA line-item license revenue and captures services revenue directly when they deliver connector buildouts on subcontract. The Epic App Orchard certification work must be led by AZOA with PrimusNeo investment backing.

PLAN Systems — AMP steward (OR command center and ED spatial)

PLAN Systems delivers the AMP spatial environment for hospital operations. The OR command center and ED throughput spatial builds are their specialty; sensor/OT integration for patient flow and equipment tracking maps to their existing capability. PLAN earns 15% of AMP line-item license revenue. Tie Town serves as the reference architecture for offline-first spatial operations — relevant for rural critical access hospitals where connectivity is unreliable.

SI channel — delivery at scale

The healthcare SI channel commands premium day rates: Deloitte Health / Accenture Health at $4,000–$8,000/day (Principal/Director); EY Health / Optum Advisory at $3,000–$6,000/day; Chartis / Huron at $3,500–$7,000/day. Epic implementation at large IDNs runs $50M–$500M in services spend. PrimusNeo engages SI channel for: (1) large IDN deployments where SI project management is buyer-required; (2) pharma RWE programs where a Big-4 advisory relationship already exists; (3) change management at scale where Chartis/Huron clinical ops credibility is needed. SI channel deals must be structured to preserve PrimusNeo’s services margin — SI markup on PrimusNeo services is typically 40–80%; structure as a reseller or co-prime model with PrimusNeo retaining direct license relationship.

Tie Town reference

Tie Town [TX] provides the reference architecture proof point for the full stack in an offline-first, privacy-preserving, TEFCA-capable deployment context. While not a hospital deployment, Tie Town demonstrates the stack’s composability for prospects who require a live reference before advancing an RFP. Until healthcare-specific lighthouse references exist, Tie Town is the fallback reference for proof-of-concept conversations.

HITRUST i1 — central investment prerequisite

HITRUST i1 certification (~$70K, recurring 3-year cycle) is a central PrimusNeo investment that unlocks the healthcare pipeline. It is not a per-engagement cost and should not be billed to any customer. It is the price of admission to substantive healthcare RFPs for 81% of hospital buyers and 83% of payer buyers. Begin immediately. Do not pursue large healthcare RFPs without it.

8. Revisions

Version Date Author Change
0.1 2026-05-23 Founding team + research squad Initial Healthcare engagement model

Logistics — Engagement Model

Vertical: Logistics (freight forwarders, 3PLs, shipping lines, port operators, last-mile, warehouse, supply chain control towers) Research brief: ../research/logistics.md Anchors: ../01-pricing-model.md, ../research/layer-comparables.md

1. The Friction-Cost Anchor

Logistics technology costs are a useful fiction. Published list prices mask the total friction: failed consortium bets, integration debt, and compliance liability that compound into a per-buyer friction window large enough to make PrimusNeo’s value capture defensible at every tier.

TMS ($400K–$3M/yr enterprise). Oracle OTM runs ~$450/user/month; enterprise TCV commonly $400K–$2M/yr. SAP TM embedded in S/4HANA estates reaches $500K–$3M/yr before implementation, which Blue Yonder alone prices at $1M–$5M. For freight forwarders, CargoWise has become the de facto operating system at $6–$10/shipment bundled — a per-shipment anchor that calibrates AZOA conversations at every tier.

Visibility platforms ($100K–$1M+/yr). project44 platform fees run $150K–$600K/yr mid-market, $1M+ at enterprise; FourKites $100K–$500K/yr. Shippers paying $300K+/yr for a visibility point solution arrive at PrimusNeo conversations already aware they’re paying for fragmented intelligence.

WMS ($500K–$3M Y1 large; $150K–$500K mid). Manhattan Active WMS at $2,000/user/yr quickly reaches $1.5M–$4M/yr for enterprise DC estates. Blue Yonder is comparable. Ongoing costs run $150K–$500K/yr after implementation.

Port/terminal OS ($300K–$1.5M/yr mid-size terminal). Navis N4-as-a-Service occupies this band; Octopi (lower-tier Navis) runs $50K–$200K/yr for simpler terminals. Awake.AI and Portchain target AI planning overlays with custom pricing.

TradeLens: the canonical failure. The November 2022 shutdown of the IBM/Maersk TradeLens consortium represents $1B+ of collectively lost value across four years. The failure was not technical — it was governance. Maersk’s perceived ownership meant MSC, CMA CGM, and Evergreen never joined; Asian carriers cited governance concerns and never trusted the platform. This is the shadow every logistics network play must address head-on.

Per-buyer friction window (worked): A national 3PL paying $200K/yr to a TMS, $150K/yr to a visibility platform, $300K/yr to a WMS, and carrying $500K/yr in EU CBAM compliance costs (effective Jan 2026) faces a $1.15M/yr coordination and compliance cost baseline before any consortium ambition. A port operator running 8 terminals — already burned by TradeLens — faces $3M–$8M/yr in visibility-spend, customs-integration, and carrier-SLA-friction across its network. These windows establish the capture band.

PrimusNeo capture band: 10–20% of consolidated platform spend (per the pricing model). At $1.15M/yr friction, a mid-tier target is $115K–$230K/yr in defensible license capture — well inside the Mid-tier pricing anchor.

2. PrimusNeo Positioning Per Layer in Logistics

The framing per layer must be precise because logistics buyers will compare against existing point solutions and will ask exactly where PrimusNeo fits or displaces.

AMP — Port/Terminal/Yard Spatial Twin + Control Tower

AMP is the terminal-and-yard spatial twin that Navis N4 describes in static data but cannot render as a live operational scene. For port operators, last-mile depot managers, and warehouse control towers, AMP provides the navigable spatial layer that ties vessel positions, crane allocations, yard slot state, inbound rail manifests, and truck gate queues into a single scene that ops teams can actually work from. AMP’s offline-first architecture matters for port edge cases (spotty connectivity on the quay, terminal edge nodes, cold-store environments). The pitch to a CSCO at a port operator: “AMP is the terminal-and-yard spatial twin — Navis N4-adjacent but vendor-neutral, and it runs on the dock.”

NEOS — Cross-Org Consortium Governance + the TradeLens Answer

NEOS is the explicit, deployable answer to the question every logistics consortium must now answer before signing: “Who owns the network?” The answer is nobody, or rather, the council does — and the council is governed by a NEOS charter that no single carrier, 3PL, or port operator controls. NEOS enforces SLA agreements, dispute resolution workflows, data-sharing policies, and contribution accounting across the consortium, with every governance event signed, anchored, and replayable. DCSA, GS1, and OpenAPI alignment is built in from day one, not retrofitted after the governance crisis.

The NEOS pitch in the carrier-shipper-3PL triangle: “No single party owns the network. You don’t trust Maersk to run TradeLens. You shouldn’t. NEOS governance means the charter owns the network — and the charter is yours, collectively, on day one.”

AZOA — Carrier-Shipper-3PL Integration Fabric

AZOA is the governance-neutral integration layer the entire carrier-shipper-3PL triangle runs across. Its critical differentiator in logistics is coverage depth: AZOA uniquely spans Web2 TMS/WMS/ERP connectors AND Web3 chain interop AND identity/SSI credentials AND IoT/device ingest under one API surface. No incumbent iPaaS (MuleSoft, Boomi, Workato) covers the Web3 + identity + IoT surface. project44 and FourKites cover the carrier-integration surface but not governance or cross-org data fabric.

Pricing mechanic: per-connector + base platform fee (default) or per-API-call/per-integration-event metered overage ($0.001–$0.05/call at volume). The CargoWise $6–$10/shipment anchor calibrates per-shipment equivalence for AZOA conversations with freight forwarders.

For the EU Maritime Single Window (effective 2025, per IMO FAL and EU regulation), AZOA is the port-operator and shipping-line integration play: pre-arrival data, port clearance, customs Single Window — all flowing through AZOA connectors rather than bespoke EDI integrations.

Fractal Engine — Predictive ETA + Emissions Optimization + Demand Sensing

Fractal is the operating intelligence layer that converts raw AMP spatial events and AZOA integration events into predictions that inform actual decisions. Three use cases anchor the logistics pitch:

  1. IMO CII compliance and voyage optimization. Vessels rated D or E for three consecutive years face mandatory corrective action. CII tightens ~2%/yr through 2026. Fractal’s voyage optimization model — fed by AMP spatial data and AZOA carrier/weather/port-call feeds — produces slow-steaming recommendations and route alternatives that close the CII gap before the next annual reporting window.

  2. EU ETS emissions cost reduction. At €60–70/tonne CO₂, large carriers face tens of millions of euros per year in ETS compliance from 2024. AMP-informed route and speed optimization, quantified by Fractal, converts into direct cost avoidance — the kind of CFO-legible ROI that unlocks budget in Q3–Q4 planning cycles.

  3. Demand sensing and ETA prediction across the global network. Fractal aggregates multi-carrier, multi-3PL, multi-port data (via AZOA ingest) into network-wide ETA predictions and demand signals that individual participants cannot produce from their own data alone. The data-value-exchange mechanic — carriers contribute raw feeds, receive aggregate benchmarking in return — is how Fractal addresses the carrier-data-hoarding rejection risk.

AZOA + NEOS is the consortium-savior duo. Neither alone solves the TradeLens problem. AZOA provides the integration fabric so data flows; NEOS provides the governance layer so the terms of that flow are trusted. Together, they are what TradeLens needed and didn’t have.

3. Engagement Tiers — Small / Mid / Large

Small: Regional 3PL / Freight Forwarder (<$50M revenue)

Profile: Regional freight forwarder or 3PL running CargoWise or a legacy TMS, handling 5K–50K shipments/yr, small compliance and IT team, 1–3 warehouse or depot sites.

Current 3-yr TCO: $120K–$400K (CargoWise subscription, EDI middleware, manual compliance, spreadsheet-based reporting).

PrimusNeo bundle: AZOA Small (TMS/customs connectors, <10 integrations) + Fractal Small (shipment ETA model, emissions reporting for CBAM, <5 data sources).

Worked quote: - AZOA Small: $40K/yr (8 integrations: CargoWise + customs authority + 3 carrier APIs + 2 shipper portals + 1 IoT tracker feed) - Fractal Small: $45K/yr (4 data sources: shipment feed, carrier API, customs data, emissions reference data) - Duet bundle: -10% → $76K/yr license - Y1 services (~2.3x AZOA-weighted blend): $175K (connector buildout: TMS $80K, customs $60K, ontology mapping $35K) - Y1 upkeep (Silver SLA, 22%): starts Y2 at $17K - Infra: $1,500/mo

Y1 total: ~$270K — 3-yr TCV: ~$480K

Friction anchor: CargoWise at $8/shipment × 20K shipments/yr = $160K/yr + manual compliance FTE at $80K/yr = $240K/yr direct. Capture: $76K ÷ $240K = 32% value share on direct displacement — aggressive, defended by the combined displacement of manual compliance + carrier EDI middleware. If the buyer retains CargoWise, frame AZOA as the enrichment layer (+ EU CBAM readiness, + ETA prediction) at 32% of the cost being displaced.

PrimusNeo target: $60K–$180K/yr; 3-yr TCV $180K–$540K (per research brief).

Mid: National 3PL ($50–500M) / Port Operator

Profile: National 3PL with regional DC network (5–10 sites), or a port operator running 3–8 terminals with cross-carrier, rail, and customs coordination needs. Existing stack: TMS, WMS, point visibility platform, legacy EDI, manual customs. EU CBAM exposure growing.

Current 3-yr TCO: $1.5M–$6M (TMS $600K–$2M, WMS $500K–$1.5M, visibility $300K–$900K, customs middleware $200K–$600K, IT/integration overhead).

PrimusNeo bundle: AZOA Mid + NEOS Mid + Fractal Mid (Triad). Port operator variant replaces Fractal with AMP Mid for terminal spatial twin.

Worked quote (national 3PL variant): - AZOA Mid: $160K/yr (20 integrations: TMS + WMS + 8 carrier APIs + 3 shipper portals + CBAM data + customs + 3 internal) - NEOS Mid: $55K/yr (3 councils: carrier SLA council, data-sharing council, compliance council) - Fractal Mid: $180K/yr (12 data sources, network-wide ETA model + CBAM emissions reporting) - Triad bundle: -18% → $323K/yr license - Y1 services (~2.0x blend): $646K (TMS connector $120K, WMS $100K, carrier connectors $200K, ontology $80K, governance design $35K, dashboards $60K + others) - Y1 upkeep (Silver SLA, 22%): starts Y2 at $71K - Infra: $8K/mo

Y1 total: ~$1.06M — 3-yr TCV: ~$2.1M

Friction anchor: TMS $900K + WMS $750K + visibility $400K + CBAM compliance cost $400K–$1.5M (definitive Jan 2026) = $2.45M–$3.55M/yr mid-range. Capture: $323K ÷ $3M = ~11% value share — inside the 10–20% logistics band.

Port operator variant (AZOA+NEOS canonical example, per layers/oas.md §7.2): AZOA Large $720K/yr + NEOS Large $220K/yr, Duet -10% → $846K/yr license. Y1 services ~2.2x: $1.86M. 3-yr TCO $6.5M–$8M. Friction anchor: TradeLens collective loss precedent + $3M–$8M/yr standing visibility + compliance friction. Capture: 17% value share.

PrimusNeo target: $300K–$900K/yr; 3-yr TCV $900K–$2.7M (per research brief).

Large: Top-20 Ocean Carrier / Top-5 3PL / Global Integrator

Profile: Tier-1 ocean carrier (Maersk, MSC, CMA CGM, Evergreen, Hapag-Lloyd) or top-5 3PL (DHL Supply Chain, Kuehne+Nagel, DB Schenker, XPO, CEVA) or global integrator (FedEx, UPS). Existing stack: proprietary TMS, WMS, visibility, and often internally built platform capabilities. EU ETS exposure: tens of millions of euros/yr. CSCO procurement cycle 9–18 months.

Current 3-yr TCO: $15M–$80M+ globally (TMS, WMS, visibility, customs, IT integration, internal-build programs).

PrimusNeo bundle: Full Stack (AMP Large + NEOS Large + AZOA Large + Fractal Large). Paid pilot ($250K–$500K) precedes full rollout.

Worked quote (top-20 carrier — Full Stack): - AMP Large: $600K/yr (port/terminal spatial twins across 6 primary hub terminals + control tower) - NEOS Large: $260K/yr (cross-carrier consortium governance: SLA, dispute resolution, data-sharing, eBL attestation councils) - AZOA Large: $800K/yr (60+ integrations: Navis N4, customs single-window, 15 carrier APIs, rail carriers, IoT vessel/container tracking, CBAM declarant flows) - Fractal Large: $900K/yr (20+ data sources: vessel telemetry, port-call data, weather, ETS pricing, customs, carrier network; CII optimization + EU ETS cost modeling + demand sensing) - Full Stack bundle: -25% → $1.92M/yr license - Paid pilot (Y0): $350K (Atom + connector audit, 90-day, credits to Y1 services) - Y1 services (~2.0x blend, minus pilot credit): $3.49M - Y1 upkeep (Gold SLA, 25%): $480K (starts Y2) - Infra: $400K–$900K/yr

Y1 total: ~$5.8M–$6.3M (including pilot) — 3-yr TCV: ~$10M–$14M

Friction anchor: EU ETS compliance at €65/tonne for a 100-vessel fleet running 2M TEUs/yr → $30M–$80M/yr ETS liability. CII corrective action plans at D/E-rated vessels: $500K–$5M per vessel-class remediation. TradeLens-pattern internal build programs: $50M–$200M sunk in failed or stalled digital platforms. Combined friction window: $50M–$150M/yr. Capture: $1.92M ÷ $100M = ~2% value share — conservative by design at this scale; the pilot ROI story (3-month CII optimization + EU ETS avoidance demo) does the commercial work before the full commitment.

Co-sell/embed motion for internally built platforms (Maersk Captain, CMA CGM CEVA, MSC One, DHL myDHLi, UPS): white-label Fractal Engine for network intelligence, or AZOA as the cross-platform integration fabric behind the carrier’s own consumer-facing product. This is not displacement; it’s a revenue-share embed.

PrimusNeo target: $1.5M–$5M+/yr; 3-yr TCV $4.5M–$15M (per research brief).

4. Industry-Specific Procurement Signals

Buyers and cycles:

Buyer Cycle Agenda
CSCO / CPO 9–18 months (large) 2024/2025 agenda: resilience + tech ROI + sustainability. TradeLens hangover: governance neutrality is table stakes before any cross-org network conversation. EU ETS + CBAM as hard financial drivers.
COO / Head of Network Ops 6–12 months (mid) Operational pain: visibility gaps, carrier SLA disputes, customs delays. Responds to demonstrated friction-cost math and peer-operator reference.
CTO / VP Engineering Integration veto AZOA connector library depth and pre-built TMS/WMS integrations are the technical proof-point. Publish connector library for top-5 TMS + top-5 WMS before enterprise sales scale.
Head of Trade Compliance / VP Customs 3–9 months (specific tooling) NEOS + AZOA for 21CCF-compliant data flows. EU CBAM declarant workflows (definitive Jan 2026). EU Maritime Single Window (2025 mandate). Responds to concrete compliance-gap analysis.

Carrier-shipper-3PL triangle dynamic: No single party controls the network. Carriers own vessel and container position data; 3PLs own last-mile and warehouse data; shippers own cargo and purchase-order data. TradeLens failed because Maersk tried to be the hub in a network where its competitors were the spokes. PrimusNeo’s answer is structural: NEOS governance is charter-owned from day one; AZOA is the neutral integration fabric; no party sees competitor raw data — only what the charter permits.

Entry motion: The NEOS + AZOA consortium-savior framing wins the first conversation with a CSCO burned by TradeLens. The CII/ETS Fractal use case wins the CFO’s attention. AMP terminal spatial twin wins the COO’s curiosity. Run them in parallel during the discovery sprint.

RFP cycles: Top-20 carriers and top-5 3PLs run formal tech RFPs annually or biannually, typically Q1 for budget alignment. Target Q3–Q4 budget season conversations to be on the shortlist before the Q1 RFP issues.

5. Rejection-Risk Mitigations

Consortium fatigue (TradeLens precedent): This is the dominant rejection risk in any cross-org logistics play. Mitigation is structural, not rhetorical: NEOS governance neutrality must be demonstrable — no single carrier may access competitor data; charter terms are cryptographically enforced, not contractually promised; DCSA/GS1/OpenAPI alignment is documented and published before the first enterprise sales conversation, not promised as a roadmap item. The correct objection-handler is not a slide — it is a signed reference charter with a neutral third-party port operator or freight association as the founding council steward.

Legacy TMS/WMS integration complexity: Oracle OTM, SAP TM, Manhattan, Blue Yonder implementations at large accounts are deeply customized. The only credible response is a published AZOA connector library for the top-5 TMS (OTM, SAP TM, Blue Yonder, MercuryGate, JDA/BluJay) and top-5 WMS (Manhattan, Blue Yonder, Körber, SAP EWM, Infor WMS) — certified, maintained, and demonstrable in a 30-day pilot window. This must exist before enterprise sales scale, not be built during each enterprise deal.

Carrier data hoarding: Ocean carriers treat real-time vessel and container position as competitive. project44 and FourKites spent years building carrier network agreements with persistent gaps. PrimusNeo’s route is a data-value-exchange model: carriers contribute raw feeds via AZOA connectors; Fractal aggregates across the network and returns benchmarking intelligence (CII performance vs peer fleet, ETA accuracy vs port benchmarks, EU ETS cost-avoidance modeling) that no individual carrier can produce from its own data. This is the model that converts a competitive instinct into a network participation incentive.

Overlap with existing visibility spend: A shipper or 3PL paying $400K/yr to project44 or FourKites is not going to add another layer on top. The conversation must position AZOA + Fractal as a superset (spatial context from AMP + governance from NEOS + intelligence from Fractal) that either displaces the point solution or absorbs it via an AZOA connector, making the total cost comparable while expanding the value surface. Displacement is the preferred outcome; augmentation is the fallback.

“We built it ourselves” at top carriers: Maersk (Captain Peter), CMA CGM (CEVA), MSC (MSC One), DHL (myDHLi), UPS — all have internal platforms. The motion here is co-sell or embed, not displacement. White-label Fractal Engine network intelligence behind the carrier’s own product; offer AZOA as the cross-platform fabric for carrier-to-shipper or carrier-to-3PL integrations that the carrier’s internal team doesn’t want to build. Revenue-share embed agreements are more likely to succeed than head-on displacement conversations at these names.

6. Regulatory + Compliance Considerations

IMO CII / EEXI (in force January 2023). Vessels of 5,000 GT and above must calculate and report their Carbon Intensity Indicator annually. A D rating for three consecutive years or an E rating triggers a mandatory corrective action plan. CII tightens approximately 2% per year through 2026. This is a direct Fractal Engine voyage-optimization use case: model slow-steaming alternatives, route options, and port-call sequences against CII targets, and surface the optimal decision in real time via the AMP control tower.

EU ETS — Maritime (2024 onward). The EU Emissions Trading System now covers 100% of intra-EU voyages and 50% of extra-EU voyages calling EU ports. At approximately €60–70 per tonne of CO₂, compliance costs reach tens of millions of euros per year for large carriers. This is the hardest financial pressure in maritime logistics today and the most compelling entry point for a Fractal + AMP ROI conversation with a CFO or sustainability officer.

EU CBAM (definitive phase January 2026). The Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism in its definitive phase requires authorized declarants for covered goods (steel, aluminium, cement, fertilizers, electricity, hydrogen) to purchase CBAM certificates tied to the EU ETS price. This creates new documentation workflows, customs classification requirements, and supplier carbon-data collection needs — all addressable by NEOS (governance of declarant workflows) and AZOA (customs and supplier data integration). National 3PLs and freight forwarders handling covered commodities must be CBAM-ready; this is a procurement trigger for the mid tier from mid-2025 onward.

US 21st Century Customs Framework (21CCF). Mandatory e-record-keeping, expanded supply chain data sharing requirements, and ACE 2.0 are in progress. Customs brokers face breach reporting obligations. AZOA is positioned for 21CCF-compliant data flows: structured, auditable, machine-readable customs data pipelines that satisfy expanded reporting requirements without bespoke broker-by-broker integration.

Single Window Mandates — IMO FAL + EU Maritime Single Window (2025). Both frameworks require pre-arrival and port clearance data submission via national single-window systems. AZOA port-operator and shipping-line integration is the direct play: standardized pre-arrival data, port state control submissions, and departure manifests flowing through AZOA connectors into the national single-window, displacing the current patchwork of EDI, email, and portal submissions.

DCSA / BIMCO eBL target (100% by 2030). The digital bill of lading sector is in pre-consolidation. WaveBL, CargoX, essDOCS, and Bolero operate at varying scales; TradeLens’ eBL function died with the platform. NEOS governance event anchoring provides a path to DCSA-aligned eBL attestation without requiring a new platform — the eBL event is a NEOS-signed, AZOA-transported document that any party in the charter network can verify.

7. Steward + Service Partner Notes

PLAN Systems (AMP steward, 15% royalty on AMP license). PLAN delivers AMP terminal/yard spatial environment buildouts — the specialized 3D scene authoring, Navis N4 data ingest, crane/berth asset modeling, and IoT binding that logistics spatial twins require. PLAN is the primary subcontract channel for AMP deployment services in logistics; their domain expertise in operational spatial environments is not replicable by a generalist SI in a short engagement window.

OmniOne (NEOS steward, 18% royalty on NEOS license). OmniOne brings the ETHOS governance-design methodology — the specific intellectual work of structuring a carrier-shipper-3PL consortium charter that no single party controls. The TradeLens-failure answer is not a product feature; it is a governance-design capability. OmniOne’s ETHOS method is the delivery vehicle for that capability. Every mid and large logistics consortium engagement should open with an OmniOne ETHOS workshop ($35K–$60K) as the first SOW line.

AZOA (AZOA steward, 15% royalty on AZOA license). AZOA’s integration depth is the commercial value. Their connector authors are the fastest path to certified TMS/WMS/customs integrations. The pre-built connector library for top-5 TMS and top-5 WMS — built and maintained by AZOA — is the technical proof-point that displaces the “integration complexity” rejection. AZOA integration engineers ($2,200/day) should be staffed on every mid and large AZOA engagement in logistics.

Escherbridge (Fractal Engine steward, 18% royalty on Fractal license). Escherbridge delivers Fractal ETA prediction, CII optimization, and EU ETS cost-modeling — the three Fractal use cases that anchor the logistics pitch. Their ML engineers ($2,800/day) build the voyage optimization models; their public corpus provides the pre-training base for network-level benchmarking. Escherbridge is the primary delivery partner for any Fractal engagement where predictive logistics and emissions analytics are the primary use cases.

Manufacturing — Engagement Model

Vertical: Manufacturing & Industrial Production Research brief: ../research/manufacturing.md Anchors: ../01-pricing-model.md, ../research/layer-comparables.md

1. The friction-cost anchor

Manufacturing is the vertical where PrimusNeo’s value proposition is most directly measurable, and most frequently stalled by pilots that never ship.

Macro anchors (sourced):

Metric Figure Source
Global manufacturing value $16.8T/yr UNIDO 2024
Global unplanned downtime cost ~$50B/yr Siemens / McKinsey
Industry 4.0 pilots that never scale 70% McKinsey
Average industry OEE 60–65% Siemens benchmark
World-class OEE target 85% Industry consensus
OEE gap × addressable sector ~20–25 OEE points Derived

The pilot-purgatory number is the dominant friction mechanism in this vertical. A plant that runs 3–6 IIoT POCs before one scales typically sinks $500K–$3M in sunk integration cost per failed pilot, with no reusable infrastructure. That sunk cost — not the software license — is what makes the first successful deployment so valuable. McKinsey’s 70% failure rate applied to the installed base of active pilots means $35B+ in annual wasted pilot spend is in play globally.

Per-customer friction windows by scale:

Scale Definition Estimated annual friction window Math
Small 1 plant, 1 line, <50 machines $500K–$2M/yr ~$1M/yr downtime (Siemens benchmark at this plant size) + $200K/yr in tools that don’t talk to each other + 1–2 stalled POCs at $150K sunk each
Mid 3–12 plants, mixed OT estate $5M–$20M/yr $50B / ~10K plants global midcap ≈ $5M/plant/yr in downtime + $1–2M/yr integration debt + pilot-purgatory sunk cost across the fleet
Large Tier-1 OEM, 25+ plants, global $50M–$200M+/yr AMP §7.2 reference: $4M/yr/plant downtime × 12 plants = $48M/yr, plus $12M+ sunk across 8 stalled POCs; scaled linearly to 25+ plants = $100M+ friction window

Capture rate target: 5–15% of the per-customer friction window per 01-pricing-model.md §9. The math must hold at every scale.

2. PrimusNeo positioning per layer in manufacturing

Manufacturing is the full-stack default vertical. Every layer has a clear and non-redundant job in the plant.

Layer Role in manufacturing Key substitutes displaced Why this beats the point solution
AMP (Spatial OS) Plant-floor spatial twin: CAD ingest, PLC telemetry binding, MES event overlay, worker location, asset-attached scene. The IT/OT bridge that makes “the plant” a navigable, offline-capable, federated data object. PTC ThingWorx ($150K–$500K+/yr/plant), Siemens Insights Hub ($50K–$300K/yr/plant), AVEVA System Platform AMP runs offline-first — no cloud dependency for the operational loop. Hexon file standard is open, so the buyer’s spatial data is never locked. Premium PLAN 3D components deliver CAD-class viewport without forcing rip-and-replace of Rockwell or AVEVA.
NEOS (Governance) Change control, management-of-change (MOC), supplier qualification attestations, CIP-013-equivalent supplier risk acceptance, signed approval logs for CAPA and FSMA traceability. Intelex, Veeva Vault QMS ($100K–$400K/yr), custom SharePoint MOC workflows NEOS produces a cryptographically signed, replayable governance log — not a PDF approval chain stored in a folder. This is what ISO 9001 / IATF 16949 auditors actually want and what the EU ESPR product-passport audit trail will require.
AZOA (Interoperability Fabric) SAP S/4HANA and ERP connectors, Rockwell FactoryTalk MES, AVEVA PI System / OSIsoft historian, Siemens MindSphere, GE Proficy, OPC-UA broker, MQTT ingest for PLC and sensor fleets. The layer that eliminates the “3 Python ETL scripts and a MuleSoft instance we hate” architecture. MuleSoft ($80K–$2M+/yr), Boomi ($6.6K–six-figure), in-house Python ETL teams (2 FTE = $300K/yr loaded) AZOA carries the full IT/OT connector library from AZOA — the connector authors who know Rockwell, Siemens, and AVEVA from the inside. Connector reuse is built into the business model, so each successive plant rollout costs less than the first.
Fractal Engine (Intelligence) Predictive OEE modeling per line and per asset, downtime root-cause classification, scrap and yield prediction, Scope 3 emissions estimation per SKU for DPP compliance, real-time signal generation from the IT/OT data fabric AZOA feeds. AspenTech Mtell ($300K–$2M/yr), C3 AI APM ($1M–$5M/yr), Cognite ($500K–$3M/yr), Sphera ESG ($150K–$600K/yr) Fractal Engine treats AMP spatial events and NEOS governance events as first-class signal types. OEE predictions are anchored to scene positions, not abstract asset IDs. Scope 3 MRV feeds directly from the production telemetry, not from spreadsheet estimates.

Full-stack is the default recommendation in manufacturing — not because we want the bigger deal, but because a twin without intelligence is a viewer, and intelligence without integration has no data. The four layers compose into a single operational fabric; selling fewer than three creates an incomplete solution that the 70% pilot-purgatory statistic will eventually claim.

Tie Town [TX] serves as the proof-of-capability reference deployment. The same AMP + AZOA + Fractal architecture used in Tie Town’s operational coordination is the template for plant-floor deployments. Buyers skeptical of a new vendor can tour the reference deployment and inspect the live Hexon spatial model before signing.

3. Engagement tiers — Small / Mid / Large

3.1 Small: 1 plant, 1 line

Definition: Single plant, 1–2 production lines, <50 machines, <100 OT-connected assets. Typical buyer is a $20M–$150M revenue manufacturer or a corporate innovation team running a line-level pilot with a defined productionization path.

Recommended bundle: AMP + AZOA + Fractal (Triad). NEOS is optional at this scale unless there is a regulatory driver (FSMA, ISO 9001 MOC, DPP pilot). If NEOS is added, it becomes a Full Stack engagement.

Friction-cost anchor for this scale: $500K–$2M/yr. Targeted capture: 8–15% = $40K–$300K/yr, justifying the license spend at payback <24 months.

AZOA Small license $36K/yr <10 integrations (SAP connector + Rockwell MES + AVEVA historian + 2 OPC-UA endpoints)
Fractal Small license $50K/yr <5 data sources (PLC telemetry, MES, historian, ERP quality module, energy meter); <1TB managed
Subtotal pre-bundle $114K/yr

Sustained upkeep (Silver SLA, 22%) $20.6K/yr Starts Y2

Capture rate check: $93.5K license ÷ $1.25M friction midpoint = 7.5% value share — inside the 5–15% manufacturing band.

Cross-reference: This is the line-level analog to the AMP Small tier in layers/amp.md §3, composed with the AZOA Mid-entry and Fractal Small anchors in layers/oas.md §3 and layers/fractal-engine.md §3.

3.2 Mid: 3–12 plants

Definition: 3–12 plants, mixed legacy and modern OT, typically $200M–$3B revenue manufacturer pursuing a digital transformation wave. Often a phased program: Wave 1 (2–3 pilot plants) → Wave 2 (remaining fleet). Average site rollout cadence: 1–2 plants per quarter after the template is established.

Recommended bundle: Full Stack (AMP + NEOS + AZOA + Fractal). At 3+ plants, MOC and supplier qualification governance (NEOS) generate enough audit surface area to justify the layer. The NEOS investment pays for itself in CAPA cycle time reduction and auditor satisfaction alone.

Friction-cost anchor for this scale: $5M–$20M/yr across the fleet. Targeted capture: 6–10% = $300K–$2M/yr.

Capture rate check: $439K license ÷ $10M friction midpoint = 4.4% value share. Low end of the band — defensible because the full-stack friction-cost displacement (downtime + pilot-purgatory + MuleSoft/integration-debt + ESG compliance) is better quantified at the discovery sprint before quoting. If downtime at 6 plants runs $3M/yr and OEE uplift from Fractal Engine is validated at 3 OEE points, capture rate on downtime alone is $439K ÷ $3M = 14.6% — top of the band.

Note on new-site rollout economics: Per the research brief, template-based additional plant deployments run $50K–$250K per plant once the reference architecture is established. The Hex SKU deployment covering the first 2–5 plants absorbs most of the architectural cost; subsequent plants use the Atom ($35K–$90K) or a scaled Molecule SKU.

Definition: Tier-1 OEM or large industrial conglomerate, 25+ plants globally, typically $3B+ revenue. Full IT/OT transformation program with SAP/Oracle ERP, multi-vendor MES (Rockwell + Siemens + AVEVA in the same fleet), OSIsoft PI historian estates, and active ESG/Scope 3/DPP obligations. COO and CFO sponsorship required. SI partner (Accenture Industry X or Capgemini) typically embedded.

Recommended bundle: Full Stack, with Gold or Platinum SLA, and likely an outcome-based addendum on OEE uplift. Tessellation deployment SKU when the program spans >6 simultaneous plant waves.

Friction-cost anchor for this scale: $50M–$200M/yr. Targeted capture: 5–8% = $2.5M–$16M/yr. The friction anchor is documented and defensible: per the layers/amp.md §7.2 reference deployment, a 12-plant discrete manufacturer runs ~$4M/yr/plant in unplanned downtime ($48M total), with $12M+ in sunk pilot POC costs and 8 stalled initiatives. At 25+ plants, the total friction window exceeds $100M/yr before ESG compliance costs are added.

With outcome-based addendum: if Fractal Engine documents 3 OEE points across 25 plants (conservative for a world-class implementation), at $10M revenue/plant/yr and 3% OEE-to-revenue correlation, documented savings ≈ $7.5M/yr. Performance fee at 20% of savings = $1.5M/yr on top of the base license. Combined effective value share: ($1.93M + $1.5M) ÷ $100M = 3.4% — well within the defensive 5–15% band and below the 2× performance-fee cap.

4. Industry-specific procurement signals

Decision makers by deal size:

Threshold Decision maker Priorities Notes
<$250K Plant Manager / VP Operations Payback <18 months, minimal IT dependency, OT-safe rollout Approves the pilot; does not approve the fleet program
$250K–$2M CIO / VP Digital / CDO Architecture fit, OT cyber posture, cloud strategy, vendor roadmap, AZOA connector coverage for the existing stack Often co-signs with Plant Manager above $500K
>$2M COO / CFO (co-sign required) Program ROI, supply-chain resilience, ESG/Scope 3 obligations, CapEx vs OpEx classification CFO increasingly focused on DPP compliance timeline post-2025
ESG / DPP scope CSO / Head of Sustainability Scope 3 accuracy, ESPR audit trail, CBAM disclosure format Enters the buying committee when Fractal Engine MRV is in scope
>$500K (formal) Procurement RFP/RFQ, MSA terms, SLA benchmarking, TCO disclosure, source-escrow availability Require binding TCO disclosure; hidden-cost discovery is the top deal-kill trigger

RFP cadences:

  • Enterprise CapEx > $5M: 6–18 month cycle. Typical flow: discovery sprint → business case → RFI → RFP → proof-of-value (paid, $50K–$150K) → vendor selection → MSA/PFEL → SOW. Accenture or Capgemini often runs the RFP process on behalf of the buyer.
  • Mid-market OpEx SaaS $500K–$2M: 4–9 months. Discovery sprint → business case → shortlist → paid POC → PO. Procurement enters formally above $500K.
  • ESG/DPP compliance-deadline-driven: 2–4 months when mandate is imminent (CBAM, DPP batteries 2025). This is the fastest procurement motion in the vertical right now.

CapEx vs OpEx bias:

Post-COVID, the dominant preference is OpEx/subscription (annual SaaS PrimusNeo ARR + services SOW) to preserve capital. OT infrastructure (edge hardware, OPC-UA gateways, historian server upgrades) remains CapEx-classified. The deal structure that wins: software OpEx (PrimusNeo ARR), connectivity CapEx (OT HW the SI or plant team procures separately), services as project CapEx (Y1 transformation budget) tapering to OpEx upkeep.

Discovery-to-PO timeline: 3–6 months for mid; 9–18 months for large. A paid discovery sprint ($40K–$80K, crediting 100% to the engagement) is the right first step at any scale. It produces a binding SOW and establishes the friction-cost baseline that makes the COO/CFO approval tractable.

5. Rejection-risk mitigations

Top rejection trigger Research brief figure PrimusNeo response
Vendor lock-in Single largest recurring rejection Hexon v1.0.0 file standard is open Apache-2.0 — the buyer’s spatial data is portable on day 1. AZOA Connector SDK is open MIT/Apache-2.0. NEOS and Fractal Engine are CC-NC — the community version is always forkable. Data export is a non-negotiable contract term (PFEL §6). Source escrow via Iron Mountain for AMP/AZOA closed-core above $500K ARR.
IT/OT integration complexity 42% cite OT integration as primary scaling barrier AZOA ships with first-class MES/OT connectors (Rockwell FactoryTalk, Siemens MindSphere, AVEVA PI, GE Proficy, OPC-UA broker) authored by AZOA — the connector authors who know these systems from the inside. The Tie Town [TX] reference architecture demonstrates live IT/OT integration. Fixed-fee connector SOWs with 90-day warranty eliminate the “integration cost overrun” failure mode.
Unclear ROI / unquantified payback 48% report buyer’s remorse from integration overruns; <24-month payback required at COO/CFO Fractal Engine predictive ROI model is instrumented in the 60-day baseline phase before the contract goes hot (per 01-pricing-model.md §8 outcome-based addendum). Downtime, OEE, and scrap baselines are established from live data before the buyer commits. Binding TCO disclosure is PrimusNeo standard in every RFP response.
Pilot-to-scale gap 70% of pilots never scale The engagement model is designed around a defined productionization path from day 1 — the Atom/Molecule/Hex/Lattice/Tessellation SKU ladder gives the buyer a fixed-fee path from pilot to fleet with known per-plant unit economics ($50K–$250K/plant for template rollouts). NEOS governance councils for the digital transformation program ensure internal buy-in is treated as a deliverable, not an assumption.
Hidden cost discovery Integration cost overruns cited by 38%; delays by 35% All connector costs are fixed-fee with explicit scope. AZOA per-connector overages are flagged in the connector-audit discovery output. Infra is always a separate pass-through line (never bundled into license), so OT hardware, historian per-tag overages, and cloud compute costs are visible before signature. Sustained upkeep at 22% Silver SLA covers connector maintenance against upstream API changes — the most common hidden-cost cliff.
Data sovereignty / cloud residency European manufacturers reject US-only cloud deployments BYO-cloud option is standard: the buyer owns the cloud account, PrimusNeo manages. AMP runs offline-first with optional EU-region cloud. Fractal Engine compute can be EU-only. NEOS coordination server is deployable on-premise or in an EU-residency cloud region. The Tie Town reference architecture supports hybrid deployments where production IP and quality data never leaves the buyer’s cloud boundary.

6. Regulatory + compliance considerations

Manufacturing is subject to the most acute near-term regulatory pressure of any PrimusNeo vertical. Three mandates are live or imminent:

EU ESPR / Digital Product Passport (DPP):

Category Mandatory DPP deadline What is required
Batteries (≥2 kWh) February 2027 (phased from 2025 pilots) Lifecycle data, Scope 3, recycled content, supply chain traceability
Textiles 2027 Material composition, origin, sustainability metrics
Electronics / ICT 2027–2029 Material, repairability score, recycled content
Industrial machinery 2029 Performance, energy, end-of-life data

PrimusNeo answer: NEOS generates cryptographically signed supply-chain attestations (supplier qualification, CIP-013-equivalent risk acceptance, incoming material traceability) that feed directly into the DPP audit trail. AZOA connects to Tier-2 and Tier-3 supplier systems to aggregate the upstream data without manual data collection. Fractal Engine computes per-SKU Scope 3 emissions from live production telemetry and outputs them in the structured formats ESPR will require. The full-stack engagement produces DPP-ready data as a by-product of normal operations — not a separate compliance project.

EU Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM):

CBAM mandatory disclosure is in force for steel, cement, fertilizer, aluminum, electricity, and hydrogen as of January 2026, with expanded scope ongoing. The mechanism requires verified Scope 3 embedded-carbon disclosure per imported tonne. For EU-importing manufacturers, non-compliance is a cost line, not just a reputational risk.

PrimusNeo answer: Fractal Engine MRV (Measurement, Reporting, Verification) signal generation from the AZOA-connected production data fabric produces CBAM-compatible embedded-carbon estimates with the audit trail CBAM verifiers require. The governance layer (NEOS) produces the attestation that the data was collected under a defined, auditable methodology — the difference between a number and a verified number.

Scope 3 mandatory disclosure (CSRD / SEC Climate Rule):

EU CSRD mandatory Scope 3 disclosure applies to ~50,000 large and listed companies in scope. Scope 3 Category 1 (purchased goods/services) and Category 11 (use of sold products) are the hardest to compute from manufacturing floor data.

PrimusNeo answer: The AZOA + Fractal Engine combination produces Category 1 supplier data directly from the AZOA-connected supply chain fabric (no spreadsheet collection), and Category 11 estimates from the AMP product lifecycle spatial model. This is the data flow that point-solution ESG platforms (Sphera, Watershed, Circularise) approximate from approximations; PrimusNeo generates it from live operational telemetry.

7. Steward + service partner notes

PLAN Systems (AMP steward): Primary delivery partner for plant-floor spatial scene work — CAD ingest, PLC telemetry binding, Hexon scene authoring, and the premium PLAN 3D industrial interface components. PLAN Systems subcontracts at the AMP Spatial/3D Engineer day rate ($2,400/day per 03-services-catalog.md §2.1). For any large engagement with significant scene-authoring scope, PLAN Systems should be named in the SOW as the primary AMP delivery sub-partner. PLAN receives 15% steward royalty on AMP license revenue per 02-licensing-model.md §4.

Escherbridge (Fractal Engine steward): Primary delivery partner for Fractal predictive maintenance model development, OEE classifier training, downtime root-cause model engineering, and Scope 3 / DPP MRV signal generation. Escherbridge ML Engineers ($2,800/day) are the right resource for the 4–8 custom model buildout that the large-tier engagement requires. Escherbridge receives 18% steward royalty on Fractal Engine license revenue.

OmniOne (NEOS steward): Primary delivery partner for NEOS change-control council design, MOC charter authoring, supplier risk governance council instantiation, and ETHOS methodology facilitation. OmniOne Governance Designers ($2,400/day) run the governance design workshop and charter authoring engagements. OmniOne receives 18% steward royalty on NEOS license revenue.

AZOA (AZOA steward): Primary delivery partner for OT/historian connector authoring (Rockwell FactoryTalk, AVEVA PI, GE Proficy, OPC-UA, MQTT), IT integration (SAP, Oracle), and the AZOA federation engine deployment. AZOA Integration Engineers ($2,200/day) carry the connector buildout work. AZOA receives 15% steward royalty on AZOA license revenue.

SI channel — Accenture Industry X / Capgemini Engineering: For large-scale Tessellation-tier programs ($5M+ TCV), co-delivery with a Tier-1 global SI is standard. Accenture Industry X and Capgemini Engineering are the recommended partners given their Industry 4.0 transformation practices and OT/MES certification pools. SI margin on implementation is 20–40%; PrimusNeo retains the license ARR and sustains upkeep revenue. SI co-delivery is the right structure when the buyer’s procurement team insists on a named SI as the primary delivery party — PrimusNeo is the software vendor and the SI is the prime implementation contractor. Introduce SI partners at the RFP stage for any deal above $3M TCV.

Robotics — Engagement Model

Vertical: Robotics (industrial, AMR, humanoid, drone, surgical, agricultural, fleet/RaaS) Research brief: ../research/robotics.md Anchors: ../01-pricing-model.md, ../research/layer-comparables.md

1. The Friction-Cost Anchor

Robotics is the vertical where the infra layer has been commoditized by the largest player in the market — and that is the opportunity. NVIDIA Isaac ROS and ROS 2 are free. AWS RoboMaker deprecated for new customers in September 2025, pushing teams to AWS Batch or direct Isaac Sim on EC2 (GPU cost ~$3–$6/hr on G6e L40S instances). This commoditization of the robot OS and simulation layer creates an acute demand for a differentiated coordination and intelligence layer sitting above it.

Comparable anchors that set the price ceiling and floor:

Vendor / Reference Model Rate
InOrbit Fleet management SaaS ~$500/robot/month (PAYG high-water; volume discounts annual)
Locus Robotics RaaS Warehouse AMR all-in (hw + sw + support + optimization) $2,000–$4,000/robot/month
Knightscope K5 Security patrol RaaS ~$60K–$70K/robot/year (~$11/hr)
Foxglove Studio Robotics observability $90+/user/month enterprise
Formant Telemetry + fleet ops Quote-based; data-egress cliff at scale
ROS 2 / NVIDIA Isaac ROS Robot OS + middleware Free (commoditized infra)
AWS RoboMaker Cloud robotics runtime Deprecated Sept 2025 for new customers
ISO 10218-1:2025 / -2:2025 cell certification TUV / Intertek / UL; 7–12 mo timeline $50K–$200K+ per certified cell
FDA 510(k) (surgical Class II) FDA fee + consulting overhead $26K–$54K + 142-day median review
EU AI Act conformity assessment (high-risk AI, Annex III) Per high-risk system $20K–$80K per system

Friction math: The direct cost of running an uncoordinated heterogeneous robot fleet is $50K–$200K+ in cert overhead per cell, 70% pilot-to-fleet failure rate (McKinsey), and $5K–$50K per unplanned downtime event in logistics contexts. A 100-robot fleet at InOrbit rates alone costs $600K/yr in fleet management software before any coordination intelligence. PrimusNeo positions the coordination/intelligence layer at $200–$800/robot/month — above the fleet-management floor ($50–$500/robot/month) and well below the full RaaS ceiling ($2K–$4K/robot/month). For a 100-robot fleet at the midpoint ($500/robot/month), that is $600K/yr for the coordination layer — a defensible add-on when positioned against the alternative: a failed pilot ($150K sunk + 12 months lost) or a costly proprietary OEM solution.

PrimusNeo capture band: 10–20% of fleet coordination friction, per the pricing model’s robotics row.

2. PrimusNeo Positioning Per Layer in Robotics

The four-layer stack maps cleanly onto the robotics coordination problem. The key positioning principle: PrimusNeo operates above the robot OS (Isaac, ROS 2, proprietary OEM stacks) and below the full RaaS operator bundle. It is the coordination intelligence tier that neither NVIDIA nor Locus nor the OEM provides.

AMP — Fleet Spatial Coordination

AMP is the operational ground layer for robotics: the multi-site spatial model that attaches robot positions, mission states, safety zones, and anomaly events to a navigable scene. In robotics vocabulary it is the fleet spatial OS that sits above VDA5050-level fleet management and below the robot proprietary controller.

  • Geofencing and safety-zone management that satisfies ISO 10218-1:2025 spatial constraints
  • Multi-site dispatch: a single spatial scene spanning 3 DCs with 200 AMRs and 2 fixed-arm cells becomes navigable and governable from one pane
  • Offline-first architecture makes AMP viable in low-connectivity environments (agricultural robots in the field, underground mining, edge-network industrial cells)
  • Composable with NVIDIA Omniverse reference architectures (AMP is not Omniverse; it is positioned as the coordination-tier complement, not the simulation replacement)

NEOS — Fleet Governance and Safety Attestation

NEOS is the governance and accountability layer for a robot fleet: the council structure that turns SLA commitments, safety-event attestations, and change-control approvals into signed, auditable, replayable records.

  • Safety attestation traceability: Every ISO 10218-compliant cell inspection, lockout/tagout approval, and HRC hazard sign-off becomes a NEOS governance event — a signed record that is cert-ready evidence, not a PDF in a SharePoint folder
  • Fleet SLA councils: For RaaS deployments, NEOS structures the operator–vendor SLA governance as a council (uptime commitments, maintenance windows, fleet rebalancing approvals) with on-chain-anchored records that support EU AI Act human oversight requirements
  • EU AI Act Annex III compliance: Fractal classified as a high-risk AI system under Annex III requires conformity assessment documentation, logging, and human oversight — NEOS provides the governance event structure that satisfies the human oversight requirement

AZOA — Vendor-Neutral Robot API Fabric

AZOA is the critical interoperability layer for robotics. Robot vendors ship proprietary controllers: FANUC KAREL, ABB RAPID, KUKA KRL, Universal Robots URScript, Boston Dynamics API, Agility Digit API. Each is a bespoke integration problem. AZOA provides the connector library and federation engine that abstracts these into one API surface.

This is the connector-buildout work that defines the robotics engagement scope. Every robot vendor added to a fleet requires a custom AZOA connector. The scope and pricing of that work is explicit (see §3 worked quotes) — it is not absorbed into the platform license.

  • Runs alongside existing NVIDIA Isaac fleet management (VDA5050) without forking the ROS graph — additive, not replacement
  • FAA Part 107 drone deployments: AZOA includes UTM (Unmanned Traffic Management) API integration as a robotics-vertical connector class
  • Post-AWS RoboMaker deprecation, AZOA positions as the vendor-neutral integration fabric for teams migrating to AWS Batch + Isaac Sim on EC2

Fractal Engine — Predictive Maintenance and Fleet Analytics

Fractal is the intelligence layer that converts robot telemetry into operational signals. In robotics it is the predictive maintenance and fleet performance analytics engine — the layer that makes a robot fleet’s operational data into a durable archive and real-time decision surface.

  • Predictive maintenance ROI model: 1 avoided unplanned downtime event in a logistics AMR fleet = $5K–$50K. A 500-robot fleet avoiding 2 incidents/month across the fleet justifies the Fractal license tier on its own
  • Custom model retraining per robot model class (AMR, fixed arm, drone) trained on customer’s sensor data; blended weights remain customer IP per the PCL default
  • Fractal audit logs are cert-ready evidence for ISO 10218 cell certification and EU AI Act conformity assessment — the Fractal telemetry archive is the paper trail TUV/Intertek/UL reviewers require
  • Per-mission metering option: $0.002–$0.01/mission event for deployments preferring consumption-based pricing over flat annual license

3. Engagement Tiers — Small / Mid / Large

Small: Startup Fleet or Single-Facility Integrator (<10 Robots)

Profile: Early-stage robotics startup deploying a pilot fleet, or a systems integrator managing a single-customer fixed installation of cobots or AMRs. Current fleet management at $0–$5K/month (free tier or entry InOrbit). Pain: no mission-level coordination, no governance, no cross-system telemetry, every ISO 10218 cert event is tracked in spreadsheets.

Bundle: AZOA Small + Fractal Small. AZOA provides the interop to ROS 2 / proprietary controller; Fractal captures telemetry and builds the predictive maintenance baseline. AMP added at renewal if multi-site; NEOS added when they need safety attestation traceability.

Worked quote (8-robot cobot cell, single facility, systems integrator):

AZOA Small license (1 domain, <1M calls/mo; 2 connectors included) $30K/yr
Fractal Small license (<5 data sources, <1TB) $45K/yr
Duet bundle discount (–10%) –$7.5K
License subtotal $67.5K/yr
AZOA connector buildout: FANUC KAREL + ROS 2 (2 connectors, fixed-fee) $55K one-time
Fractal data pipeline buildout (2 sources: PLC + historian) $40K one-time
Atom deployment SKU (single site, 2 layers) $50K one-time
Y1 services total $145K
Sustained upkeep Silver SLA (22% of license) $14.8K/yr (starts Y2)
Infra pass-through + 12% mgmt fee ~$800/mo (~$9.6K/yr)

Y1 total: ~$222K. 3-yr TCO: ~$290K–$320K (services taper Y2–Y3).

Friction anchor: ISO 10218 cert-prep spreadsheet labor ($15K–$40K/cell), InOrbit or equivalent fleet mgmt at $0–$3K/month, 1 avoided downtime incident/yr ($10K–$30K value). Capture rate: $67.5K license ÷ estimated $80K annual friction displacement = ~84% of direct friction displacement — high, because this buyer has almost nothing today and the connector buildout is real capability they could not otherwise buy.

Mid: Multi-Site Fleet Operator or Mid-Size OEM/Integrator (50–500 Robots)

Profile: Mid-size logistics operator running 200 AMRs across 3 DCs, or a Tier-2 OEM integrator delivering fleet-as-a-service to 5 customers with heterogeneous robot vendors. Current spend: InOrbit or Formant $25K–$250K/yr + custom SI $200K–$800K. Pain: 3 different robot vendors (KUKA, Universal Robots, a domestic AMR brand), multi-site dispatch without a unified spatial model, safety audit readiness before an ABB/FANUC partnership requires ISO 10218 attestation, no predictive maintenance.

Bundle: AMP Mid + NEOS Mid + AZOA Mid + Fractal Mid (full stack). AMP handles multi-site spatial coordination. NEOS handles fleet SLA governance and safety attestation councils. AZOA handles the heterogeneous vendor API surface. Fractal handles predictive maintenance and analytics.

Friction anchor: Current InOrbit/Formant + SI spend: $250K–$800K/yr. 70% pilot failure rate means 1 failed pilot at this scale costs $100K–$300K in sunk SI + 9–12 months of delay. Predictive maintenance: 4 avoided AMR downtime events/month across 3 DCs × $15K/incident = $720K/yr in avoided logistics disruption. ISO 10218 cert-prep per cell: $75K avg per cell × 3 cells = $225K one-time displacement. Capture rate: $416K ÷ estimated $1.4M annual friction = ~30% value share — defensible inside the 10–20% capture band for robotics (the higher number reflects displacement of real line-items, not just advisory uplift).

RaaS opex framing: “You’re already paying $2,500/robot/month to your AMR vendor all-in. We add $416K/yr ÷ 200 robots = $173/robot/month for coordination intelligence, safety attestation traceability, and predictive maintenance that cuts unplanned downtime 15%. That is a $208/robot/month net gain on your existing $2,500 RaaS invoice.”

Large: Global OEM or National Logistics Fleet (1,000+ Robots)

Profile: Global robot OEM (ABB, FANUC, KUKA, Boston Dynamics class) seeking to offer a coordination intelligence platform as an embedded SKU in their fleet management software, OR a national logistics operator (Symbotic class) running 1,000+ AMRs across 20+ facilities. Current spend: proprietary OEM fleet software + SI ($1M–$5M+/yr); custom digital twins at $500K–$3M. Pain: cross-vendor fleet interop (EU sites run KUKA; US sites run Fanuc; AMR layer is Symbotic; surgical arm in one facility is Intuitive Surgical); global compliance (ISO 10218:2025 across all cells, EU AI Act by 2026 enforcement, FDA SaMD layer for any clinical robotics); no unified fleet analytics.

Bundle: Full stack at Large tier. AZOA federation engine on. NEOS federated governance councils per region. AMP multi-org spatial federation. Fractal large with custom model retraining per robot class.

Title Authority Entry Point
